# Deferred findings

Where out-of-spec issues go the moment they are found, instead of being fixed on
the spot or forgotten. Anything discovered while a milestone is in flight that is
not required by that milestone lands here as one row: what, where, the evidence
it is real, and a suspected severity.

**Nothing here is scheduled work.** The owner reviews each row and approves it
individually; approved rows become the phase's final milestone. An unreviewed row
is a report, not a commitment — which is what keeps "I noticed something" from
turning into unplanned scope.

Issues that make the *current* milestone's own claim false are in spec by
definition and get fixed immediately, whatever subsystem they turn up in. The
test is the claim, not the file.

**A row never just disappears.** It moves to *Closed* naming what closed it, or
it acquires a milestone number in [Plan.md](../../Plan.md), or its removal is an
entry in [decisions.md](decisions.md) saying it was dropped and why. Deciding a
finding does not matter is a decision, and one made silently is indistinguishable
from a row somebody deleted by accident. This is workflow.md's standing rule; it
is repeated here because this is the file most tempting to tidy.

The process around this — when to defer, what counts as work, what has to pass
before a phase PR — is in [workflow.md](workflow.md).

---

## Open

**Found in** names the milestone that was under way when the row was written —
not necessarily the milestone that caused the defect, which the evidence names
when it is known and different. It is written by whoever appends the row, at the
time, because it is unrecoverable afterwards.

| # | Found in | Finding | Where | Evidence | Severity | Reviewed |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| F75 | M44 | Revoking an API key is scoped to the key's id and its **owner**, with no organization check, while listing keys is scoped to the owner *and* the organization — so the two disagree about which keys an actor acting in one organization can reach | `internal/store/query/apikeys.sql`, `RevokeAPIKey` (`WHERE id = $1 AND user_id = $2`) against `ListAPIKeysForUser` (`WHERE user_id = $1 AND organization_id = $2`); the caller is `internal/auth/apikey.go` `Revoke`, which passes only `actor.UserID` | Read from the two statements, not reproduced. A user belonging to two organizations sees only the current organization's keys in the list, but a `DELETE /api/v1/api-keys/{id}` naming a key issued in the other one answers `204` and revokes it. Both keys are that user's own, so nothing crosses a tenancy boundary and no key is destroyed that its owner could not have destroyed by switching organization first. What it does give is a weak existence oracle — `204` versus `404` distinguishes *my key, elsewhere* from *not mine* — against ids the caller would have to guess. Not touched by M44: rotation adds no revoke path, and the milestone's org-wide keys are still revoked by their own owner | **Low.** An inconsistency between two statements of the same scoping rule, with a negligible oracle attached. The fix is one `AND organization_id = $3`, and the argument against it is that it would make a key unrevokable from the organization somebody is signed into, which is worse | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. **Re-homed 2026-08-05, owner-directed, and still open.** The owner's answer to this row is a change of model rather than a filter: a key minted by an *account*, reaching the organizations that account belongs to, GitHub-style. Validated as dissolving this row — revoke and list would both be owner-scoped, so they agree by construction instead of by a filter that makes a key unrevokable from the organization somebody is signed into, which this row's own severity calls worse than the defect. **Not built in M45**: it reverses [F103](#open)'s bound, M44's `organization_id` parameter on `ResolveWorkspaceForUser`, D43's role cap and D87's rotation rule, so it is a tenancy model changing rather than a defect being fixed, and a phase close is not where that happens. Scheduled as a *Not in Phase 2* row in [Plan.md](../../Plan.md), which carries the work it implies. This row stays **open** until that lands: scheduled elsewhere is not resolved. |
| F90 | M44.9 | The webhook delivery queue is 40 rows a minute for the whole instance, strictly arrival-ordered, with no per-tenant bound | `internal/store/query/webhooks.sql:110-117` — `WHERE status='pending' AND next_attempt_at <= now() ORDER BY next_attempt_at LIMIT $batch_size`, no workspace term; `DrainBatch = 20` on a 30-second tick; `MaxWebhooksPerWorkspace = 20` (`internal/domain/webhook.go:113`) | One link write in a workspace holding the permitted twenty subscriptions produces twenty rows, so two link writes a minute from one workspace saturate instance-wide capacity. `EnqueueWebhookDeliveries` applies no cap on pending rows and `WEBHOOK_RETENTION_DAYS` prunes only *finished* ones, so a backlog is unbounded in size and duration; every row a tenant queues has an earlier `next_attempt_at` than everything queued after it. The query file's own header (`:5-9`) argues the tenant term was omitted deliberately — *"a drainer that filtered by tenant would deliver in tenant order, and the queue is fair or it is a queue somebody's backlog can starve"* — and removing it produced arrival-ordered delivery, which starves the same way | **Moderate**, rising on a multi-tenant instance where one customer's ordinary traffic silently delays another's integration. No loss and no disclosure — rows arrive late. The headline capacity is low enough to be a design fault rather than only an abuse case: one customer creating links through the API at 40/min with a single subscription reaches the ceiling with no attacker involved. Compounds F82. Fix note: `DISTINCT ON (workspace_id)` or a round-robin term reintroduces the tenant-ordered delivery the file refuses **and** cannot sit under `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`; a per-workspace pending cap silently drops events, which Plan.md already says nothing recovers | **Reviewed 2026-08-05 and deliberately carried**, M45's third triage, Tier 1. Not approved as work and not closed: the row stays open and the defect is published as a *Known limitations* row in [Plan.md](../../Plan.md). The reason is **not** cost. This row has no repair — its own fix note disposes of both obvious mechanisms, since `DISTINCT ON (workspace_id)` reintroduces the tenant-ordered delivery the query file refuses *and* cannot sit under `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, while a per-workspace pending cap silently drops events Plan.md already says nothing recovers. What is left is choosing a fairness model for a queue, which is a design decision, and a phase close is the worst point in this project's calendar to take one. |
| F141 | M45 | **There is no account recovery in this product at all.** A user who forgets their password is locked out permanently, whether or not a mailer is configured, and nothing anywhere says so | No route: `grep` for `forgot`, `reset`, `recover` across `internal/httpx/router.go` returns nothing. No machinery: no `password_reset` table, column, token or query anywhere in `internal/` or `internal/store/migrations/`. No template: `internal/ui/templates/mail/` holds four — `invitation`, `verification`, `dispute-decided`, `audit-growth`. The only password write is `POST /account/password` (`internal/httpx/router.go:482`), behind `signedIn` | Found while validating the owner's question on [F140](#open) — *is account recovery possible as long as a mailer is set up*. The answer is no, and the mailer is irrelevant: the mechanism does not exist to be enabled. Three things make it worse than an absent feature. **Plan.md does not list it** in *Not in Phase 2* or anywhere else, so it is not a deferred decision, it is a gap nobody recorded. **The product has every part needed to build it** — a token-hash pattern used twice (invitations, signup verification), an outbox, a mailer, and argon2 rehashing on `POST /account/password` — so its absence reads as an oversight rather than a choice. And it **compounds F140**: D98's instance principal is one account, that account's only credential is a password, and losing it now strands the instance with `psql` as the sole route back, which is precisely what F140 is about | **Moderate to major.** No data loss and no disclosure, and an operator with database access can always rewrite a hash — so it is an availability and product-completeness defect rather than a security one. It is the highest tier of that class: on an instance with real users, the first forgotten password is a support request the product cannot answer, and there is no workaround short of the operator editing the database on somebody's behalf, which is also the only route to re-appointing a lost instance principal | No, and deliberately still no. Reviewed 2026-08-05: the owner chose to **record the absence and schedule nothing**, so this row is now documented in the two places a reader looks — Plan.md's *Not in Phase 2* and README's *Not built yet* — and each says the concrete thing rather than the abstract one: a forgotten password cannot be recovered by the person who forgot it, a mailer does not change that because no mechanism exists to use one, and the operator's route is editing the database. Both name `lctl instance principal move` as covering the instance principal and nothing else. **The row stays Open**: it is documented, not resolved, and the first limb of this cell's own argument — that Plan.md does not list it — is the only one that has stopped being true |

## Closed

A row leaves *Open* when the work that makes it untrue has landed, and the
milestone that closed it is named. The finding itself is not edited — the
evidence is what it was, and rewriting it would hide the shape of the defect
from anyone reading back.

A row also leaves *Open* when it is **merged** into another row carrying the same
defect. Merging is not deleting: the row moves here untouched, its *Closed by*
names the survivor, says what was folded in and what — if anything — was left
behind, and the survivor's *Reviewed* cell names it back. Two rows merge only
when one edit closes both. Two distinct defects that merely share a cause stay
apart and cross-reference each other instead, which is the more common answer.

| # | Found in | Finding | Where | Evidence | Severity | Reviewed | Closed by |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| F1 | 0.1.0 release | Release-notes extraction sweeps up the changelog's link-reference block | `.github/workflows/release.yml`, the "Extract this version's changelog section" step | The `awk` runs from `## [<version>]` to the next `## [` heading. The newest version is always the last section in `CHANGELOG.md`, so the trailing reference definitions are inside that range: the published v0.1.0 body carries `[Unreleased]: …` and `[0.1.0]: …` immediately before the `### Container image` section. Confirmed against the live release body, not inferred. | Cosmetic. Markdown renders link *definitions* as nothing, so a reader sees no artefact; the cruft is in the raw body only. It will recur on every release, because the newest section is last every time. | Yes — owner approved 2026-07-31, scheduled as [M45](phase-details/m45.md), which is the only milestone it can sit in: the extraction runs solely inside the release workflow, so the fix has to be checked against CHANGELOG.md as it will be at 0.2.0 before the tag exists rather than confirmed by publishing.  **Fix written and verified 2026-08-06, waiting on the owner to apply it.** It lands in `.github/workflows/release.yml`, which the agent's token may not push — see [W29](workflow-changes.md) and `ci/proposed/release.yml`. The row stays **open** rather than closed on a written fix: the extraction that runs at the 0.2.0 tag is the live workflow, not the proposal, so until it is applied the release body carries the link definitions exactly as v0.1.0's does.  **Corrected 2026-08-06, and it lowers the urgency I claimed.** The defect bites only the section that is *last* in `CHANGELOG.md`, because that is the only one whose range runs off the end into the reference block. v0.1.0 was hit because it was the first release and therefore last in the file. Once `[0.2.0]` was cut, `[0.1.0]` sits beneath it — and both the old program and the new one extract 0.2.0 with **zero** reference definitions, measured against the real file. So this does **not** block the 0.2.0 release, and my earlier statement that the release body would carry them was wrong. The fix stays proposed and worth applying: it is defensive against a future release that becomes last, and against anyone reordering the file. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-06, applied by the owner as `447095c` — the fix is in `.github/workflows/release.yml` and the row closes on the live workflow rather than on the proposal, which is the distinction it stayed open for. Verified against the real `CHANGELOG.md` by running the live program over **both** released sections: 0.2.0 gives 1802 lines and 0.1.0 gives 164, each with zero stray reference definitions and no trailing blank. **The urgency I claimed was wrong and is corrected above**: the defect bites only whichever section is last in the file, so 0.2.0 was never going to carry them and this was defensive rather than blocking. One flaw surfaced on the way in and is **not** fixed here: the proposal was a whole-file copy with a `# PROPOSAL` header, so applying it put that header on the live workflow, which now opens by telling a reader to apply a file they are already reading. Removing it is itself a workflow change the token cannot push — [W30](workflow-changes.md), proposed. It is cosmetic and F1 closes without it, because what F1 asserts is what the `awk` extracts. |
| F144 | W27 | CI's integration job runs a strict subset of what `make test-integration` runs, so one integration test never runs on a runner at all | `Makefile`, `test-integration` — `go test -race -tags=integration ./test/integration/... ./cmd/lctl/... ./cmd/linkctrl/...`; `Makefile`, `ci-integration` — `go test -tags=integration -race -count=1 ./test/integration/`, which is the whole of what the workflow's *Integration tests* step now runs | Read from the two commands, not reproduced. The Makefile's own comment above the target names what the divergence loses: *"cmd/lctl carries one integration test of its own: the demo coverage list (M33.5), which has to seed a database to know whether the demo shows anything. It cannot live in test/integration, because that package cannot import package main."* So the demo-coverage test is gated locally and ungated in CI, and a change that breaks it merges green. Found while moving the CI steps into make targets (W27); **not fixed there**, because widening CI's coverage inside a refactor is exactly the change-mixing that the proposal route exists to prevent — the fix is one path added to `ci-integration`, which is a Makefile edit and needs no workflow proposal | **Low.** No user-facing defect and nothing exploitable: the test exists and passes locally, and `make check` plus the pre-commit gate still run it before anything lands. What is missing is the backstop — a commit that skipped the gate, or a dependency change that breaks only that test, reaches `main` with CI green. Cheap to close and cheap to leave | **Approved as work 2026-08-06**, owner-answered at M45. W28's split already puts a changed check in a make target, so the mechanism needed nobody; the approval is what it needed. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-06. `ci-integration` runs the same three packages `test-integration` does. **Two tests, not one**: the row names `cmd/lctl`'s demo-coverage list, and `cmd/linkctrl` had joined it earlier the same session — [F73](#closed) added the scheduler's first tests there, for a job whose entire claim is that it runs without leadership, and CI had never run them. Neither package can live in `test/integration`, because that package cannot import package main, which is precisely why both were easy to leave out of a CI command and hard to notice missing. Mirrored in the Taskfile. Verified by running the widened target: three packages green, the demo seeder adding ~23s. |
| F143 | no milestone (W25) | [decisions.md](decisions.md)'s index no longer matches its file order, which its own header requires. The rule is stated there in four words: *Newest last, matching the file* | [decisions.md](decisions.md), index row at `:192` against the heading at `:16534` | The index reads `… four controls · four small rows · A command that chooses a loop · Two stops · The second stop · Describing the commands`. The body reads `… four controls · A command that chooses a loop · Two stops · The second stop · Describing the commands · four small rows`. **Cause is two sessions appending to one append-only file**, not either commit being wrong on its own: the W11–W12 process entries merged into `phase-2` at `ec8b1e2`, index and body consistent; M45's next entry then appended its body after them and inserted its index row after M45's previous one, which was correct against the index M45 had read. Every link still resolves, so `make check-links` passes and nothing mechanical sees it | Low. Navigation only — no entry is missing, none is wrong, and no anchor is broken. It matters because the file's ordering rule is the one thing making an append-only log readable in the order things happened, and a reader who trusts the index gets the sequence of two 2026-08-05 sessions backwards. The fix is moving one row; the interesting half is that **nothing gates it** — same family as F20 in StrucGu, a rule the commit gate cannot see | No | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Closed under the standing approval for documentation corrections rather than by being scheduled: the index is navigation, and correcting it changes nothing the product does. **Rebuilt rather than patched**, because the gap was larger than the row knew — 22 entries were missing, three of them predating this session, and the surviving rows were no longer in file order either, so appending the missing ones would have produced a complete index that still broke its own rule. Every existing row is preserved verbatim; only the order changed and the 22 gained descriptions. Verified by re-deriving both sequences from the file and comparing them element for element — 201 headings, 201 rows, identical order — rather than by reading it, since counting a list by eye is what this file has already been caught doing twice. |
| F17 | M32.9 | `docs/SECURITY.md`'s paragraph on what the audit log records is wrong in both directions — it under-reports what is recorded, over-promises what is coming, and contradicts itself | `docs/SECURITY.md:299-306`, the paragraph beginning "**The audit log works, but plenty still does not write to it.**" — the enumeration at `:301-304`, the promise at `:304-306` — against `internal/audit/audit.go` and its comment at `:211-214` | Found by M32.9's documentation-truth dimension. **Limb one, under-reporting** (`:301-304`): the paragraph says "The events recorded today are" — present tense, no as-of scoping — and lists the root redirect, the invitation lifecycle and M28's membership, workspace and organization changes. It omitted five actions that had shipped by M32.9: `destination.blocked` (M30), `dispute.allowed` and `dispute.upheld` (M31), and `link.bot_blocking_changed` and `domain.bot_blocking_changed` (M32.5) — 5 of the 17 that existed then, and the vocabulary has since grown to thirty-one. Adversarial check: the same file names `destination.blocked` at `:221` and `:251`, so SECURITY.md contradicts itself rather than merely lagging. **Limb two, over-promising** (`:304-306`, folded in from F112, M44.9): the same paragraph says key minting and revocation *"arrive with the Phase 2 milestone that owns it"*, while `internal/audit/audit.go:211-214` says their absence *"is **not an oversight**: both require an interactive session, so the person is the record"*. M44 owned the key lifecycle, shipped, and added only `apikey.rotated`. No decision entry records either position, so the document is not describing a recorded choice — it promises a capability the code has refused. No milestone's own definition of done is falsified, which is why this is a row and not an in-spec fix | Minor to moderate. Documentation only, but it is the file a security-conscious reader trusts most, and it is wrong in **both** directions in one paragraph: under-reporting what exists, and promising what the code has refused. A compliance reader defers building their own key-lifecycle logging waiting for something that already came and went | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 2, minor to moderate. This **supersedes the 2026-08-01 deferral** rather than sitting beside it: that review covered limb one, and limb two arrived afterwards by absorbing F112, so it had never been reviewed at all. Reviewed 2026-08-01: owner agreed the M32.9 triage — deferred, not scheduled. Agreeing a row is deferred is not approving it as work; it stays here until a milestone claims it. F17 and F18 are the natural pair for M45's documentation pass. **[F112](#closed) (M44.9) was merged into this row** by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04 — same paragraph, one edit, and F112 called itself *"a second limb of F17"*. The 2026-08-01 deferral covered limb one only; limb two arrived after it and has had no owner review. Deliberately **not** merged with [F45](#open): same cause, different file and different sentence — see F45's Reviewed cell | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05, after [F18](#closed) rather than before it — F18 built `audit.AllActions`, and rewriting this paragraph without it would have been a fourth hand-count. Both limbs are corrected. **Under-reporting:** it named five kinds of event against a vocabulary of thirty-two, and it no longer restates a list at all — it points at `AllActions`, whose length is the coverage row above and whose completeness a test enforces. **Over-promising:** *each arrives with the Phase 2 milestone that owns it* is gone, and so is the claim that key revocation is untrailed, which `apikey.revoked` had made false since M44. What replaces both is a checked list of what genuinely is not audited — link writes, sign-in, minting a key, and the account a redemption may create — each verified against `AllActions` rather than remembered. The paragraph now says it was wrong in both directions and why, because a security document that quietly corrects itself gives a reader no reason to re-check the next claim. |
| F19 | M32.9 | "May an invitation create an account" is derived independently in two places, and only one of them is the enforcement | `internal/httpx/web_invitations.go:152` re-derives it as `Config.Auth.SignupMode != config.SignupClosed`; enforcement is `internal/invite/invite.go:572` reading `cfg.NewAccounts`, set at `cmd/linkctrl/main.go:413` from `signupSvc.Effective().AdmitsNewAccounts()` | Found by M32.9. **The refutation partly succeeded and the finding is narrowed accordingly**: the two derivations agree in all four reachable states today, because `Effective()`'s only derivation is open-with-no-mailer becoming `invite`, which still admits accounts. So this is NOT a live defect and no user is currently misled. What remains is that D7's rule has two implementations, and the page's copy bypasses the effective-mode logic entirely — if `Effective()` ever gains a case yielding `closed`, the invitation page would invite somebody to register while redemption refuses them. | Minor, latent. Nothing is wrong today; the cost is a second implementation of a rule the project treats as absolute. | Reviewed 2026-08-01: owner agreed the M32.9 triage — deferred, not scheduled. **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, owner-answered at M45. Nothing is wrong today; what is bought is one statement of a rule the project treats as absolute, before the two derivations drift. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. The redemption page asks `invite.Service.AdmitsNewAccounts` instead of re-deriving the rule from `Config.Auth.SignupMode`. The two were not merely duplicated, they were **different questions that agree**: the page read the *configured* mode and enforcement reads the *effective* one, and `open` with no mailer degrades to `invite`. A nil receiver answers false, so a caller with no invite service shows no form for an account it cannot create. **Finding the test uncovered a second gap**: `Deps.Web` was never set in the invite fixture, and `GET /invite/{token}` hangs off `web.Invites` — so the redemption page had no test of any kind, and no assertion about what somebody holding a link sees was even reachable. The fixture serves the dashboard tree now. The test sets the two answers apart deliberately, which `cmd/linkctrl` cannot produce because it derives one from the other; that is the point, since what is asserted is that the page *cannot* disagree with the enforcer rather than that today's wiring keeps them equal. Sabotaged by restoring the second derivation. |
| F21 | M25 | Picking a workspace in the header dropdown does nothing until **Switch** is pressed, and any navigation silently discards the choice | `internal/ui/templates/partials/nav.html:23`–`:35` — a bare `<select>` and a separate submit button, with no change handler | Found by adversarial verification of two owner reports that a selection *resets* between pages and that the links page *does not update*. Both are this: the store-level switch is sound (`POST /workspace/switch` persists `users.last_workspace_id` and survives new sessions, 500 pages, API calls and short-link hits), but the UI never sends that POST unless the button is pressed, so the next render re-emits `selected` at the workspace the user is actually in and their pick vanishes. The template's own comment records what it believed was the cause — an auto-submitting select needs a change handler and the CSP forbids inline script — so this reads as a deliberate design whose affordance misleads, not an oversight. It is what the owner actually experienced. **That justification is false, and the tree falsifies it** (checked 2026-08-02, draining the queue): the CSP forbids *inline* script and always did, but htmx has been served from `internal/ui/static/js/htmx.min.js` since M11 (`13fb0f6`), is loaded by `layout.html:9` on every page, and runs under `script-src 'self'` — `internal/httpx/middleware.go:266-270` says so in as many words. `internal/ui/templates/pages/links.html:56` already ships **exactly** the control this finding says was impossible: `<select name="status" hx-get="/links" hx-trigger="change">`, needing no inline script and no CSP waiver. So the two-step was never forced by the CSP; it was a change handler nobody wired | Moderate. Nothing is broken or lost, and D22's persistence holds — but a claim *is* false, which is new: `nav.html`'s comment asserts a constraint the same codebase disproves one directory over, and that comment is what would stop the next reader from fixing this. The cost is that the control looks like it changed something and did not, which is indistinguishable from a bug to the person using it | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 1, moderate and above. The owner directed the fix's shape on 2026-08-02; what it still waits on is the no-JavaScript question in [upcoming-decisions.md](upcoming-decisions.md). but the **owner has directed the shape of the fix** (2026-08-02, via `/note`): the dropdown performs the switch on change, and *"a separate button to switch can't stay"*. The one thing that leaves open — what a browser without JavaScript gets once the button is gone — is in [upcoming-decisions.md](upcoming-decisions.md). Approval to schedule the work is still the owner's and has not been given | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05, under **D103**. The select carries `hx-post`, `hx-trigger="change"` and `hx-include="closest form"`, and the **Switch** button is gone — the owner's 2026-08-02 directive carried literally. No `<noscript>` fallback: the owner settled the stance instead, so the dashboard requires JavaScript and that is written down rather than defended in markup. **Nothing was added to make it work.** htmx is already served on every page under `script-src 'self'` and already does this on the links page, and `seeOther` already answers an htmx request with `HX-Redirect`, so the switch performs a full navigation whichever way it was submitted and the handler is untouched. `nav.html`'s comment — *a plain form: a select and a button, no JavaScript*, justified by a CSP that forbids inline script — went with the button: the premise was false, since none of this needs inline script, so the two-step control was defending a constraint that did not exist. Asserted on the markup, because everything past the emitted attributes belongs to htmx and the browser; sabotaged by removing the wiring, which fails on all three attributes. |
| F18 | M32.9 | The audit action vocabulary is split across two packages, so anything enumerating it from `internal/audit` silently misses two actions | `internal/dispute/dispute.go:113`–`:114` define `ActionDisputeAllowed` and `ActionDisputeUpheld`; the other fifteen are in `internal/audit/audit.go` | Found by M32.9. Adversarial check confirmed both actions are emitted and written correctly, so no behaviour is wrong today and this is structural only. It is the mechanical cause of F17: a reader listing the vocabulary from `audit.go` gets fifteen and believes that is all of them. The next milestone to add an audit surface has a coin-flip precedent to follow. | Minor. No incorrect behaviour; the cost is that the vocabulary has no single home, and every consumer that enumerates it is wrong by two. | Reviewed 2026-08-01: owner agreed the M32.9 triage — deferred, not scheduled. **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, owner-answered at M45 alongside [F17](#open): the split vocabulary is the mechanical reason a count taken from `internal/audit` is wrong by two, and that count has been wrong twice in `docs/SECURITY.md`. Unifying it before the documentation pass makes the number it writes checkable rather than hand-counted. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `ActionDisputeAllowed` and `ActionDisputeUpheld` are declared in `internal/audit` and referred to from `internal/dispute`, so the vocabulary has one home. **The rehoming is half the fix and the smaller half**: `audit.AllActions` is the enumeration nothing could write before, and `TestAllActionsIsExhaustive` parses `audit.go` and fails if a constant is declared without appearing in it — so adding an action and forgetting the list is a failing build rather than a documentation defect found two milestones later. Reflection cannot see constants, so the list is written out; a written list is only safe if something checks it. Thirty-two declared, thirty-two listed, and `docs/SECURITY.md`'s count is that length rather than a number kept in step by hand. Its explanation of *why* the count came up short is corrected with it — the parenthetical saying the two live in `internal/dispute` is what this row just made false. Sabotaged by dropping one entry: it fails by name and again on the length, which is the check that catches a duplicate. |

| F14 | M30 | Anyone who can create a link can now write audit rows at will, because every refused destination is recorded | `internal/link/blocking.go` — `Service.recordBlocked`, reached from `checkDestination` on every tiered refusal, on all three destination-writing surfaces | Built into M30 by its own bullet: *every blocked attempt writes an audit event with `ip_prefix` and the attempted URL stored as evidence*. The caller needs `links.create` or `links.update`, so this is not reachable anonymously — but on an instance with open signup (M29) or many members, a holder of an ordinary editor role can loop `POST /api/v1/links` with `http://127.0.0.1/` and add a row per request, each carrying a metadata blob and up to 2 KiB of defanged URL. Nothing rate-limits the write beyond `LINKCTRL_API_RATE_PER_MIN` (600/min by default), and unlike the other eleven audit actions this one records something that did *not* happen, so there is no successful state change bounding how often it can be provoked. | Minor, and bounded rather than unbounded: M21 shipped `LINKCTRL_AUDIT_RETENTION_DAYS` and `LINKCTRL_AUDIT_SIZE_WARN_BYTES`, so growth is trimmed and an operator is warned — but retention defaults to `0` (keep forever, D5), which is the configuration where this costs disk indefinitely. The trail is worth more than the risk, which is why the bullet is right and this is a row rather than a design change; the candidate fixes — a per-actor rate on the record, or collapsing repeated identical refusals into one row with a count — are both behaviour changes to a shipped audit contract and belong to whoever schedules them. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 3, minor — a principal can act on it, in scope by the 2026-08-04 standing rule. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `recordBlocked` is bounded per actor **and per refusal code**, ten a minute (`BlockedAuditRatePerMin`), through the same limiter set every other limit is built in — shared via Redis so the bound means one thing across replicas, falling back to local buckets, which errs toward more rows rather than fewer. **The per-code half is the fix, not a refinement**: a per-actor budget would be a suppression tool, because the attacker picks the noise and could bury the refusal that mattered under a flood of a different one. A code nobody has provoked before is always recorded. The bound is on the *row* and never on the block — a destination is refused identically whether or not its audit row is written, and the test asserts that too. Of the two candidates this row named, collapsing repeats into one row with a count was rejected: `audit_logs` is append-only by design and mutating a written row is a larger break of that contract than declining to add one. **[M30](phase-details/m30.md)'s bullet is amended, not silently falsified** — it read *Every blocked attempt writes an audit event*, and the amendment carries the bullet as it stood, as amended, and the tree fact. `docs/SECURITY.md`'s audit row says the same in operator terms. Sabotaged twice: keying per actor alone fails the burying assertion, and removing the bound writes twelve rows for twelve identical refusals. |

Not a scoping defect and not wrong data — two defensible definitions of *clicks* rendered adjacently with nothing distinguishing them | Minor. A plausible source of somebody reporting that the dashboard numbers look wrong | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Both figures are labelled rather than made equal, because both are correct and the dashboard needs both: the tile is the 30-day human figure the page is about, and the per-link number is the all-time total the sort order and the links page use. **The two differ on two axes, not the one this row names** — bots *and* the window — so labelling only the bot half would have left a reader with a different unexplained gap. *Recent links* now says *all time, bots included* beside the heading, each per-link count carries the full reason in its title, and the *Clicks* tile gains a marker saying bots are counted separately and the per-link totals are larger by design. No number moved: this is F107's answer applied to the same shape of problem — two defensible definitions rendered next to each other, where the defect is the silence between them rather than either figure. |

The clamp's error self-corrects within fifteen minutes; the zero-visitors column does not correct at all, and the row's own evidence says so — a figure that is permanently wrong under a heading reading *"attributed to the destination that served them"* is a wrong number rather than a late one, and nothing on the panel distinguishes the two. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 2, minor to moderate. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05, owner-directed: *keep the row, mark it approximate*. | F73 | M40 | The verified-hostname cache has **no periodic reload**, so a replica that misses a pub/sub message serves a stale set until it restarts or hears another one | `internal/redirect/hosts.go` — `Reload` is called at boot, on `kindHost`, and by the subscriber's `flush` on every (re-)establishment; nothing is on a timer. The alias tier does not need one because every entry carries `REDIRECT_TTL`; this set has no TTL by construction, which is what makes it exact | Reproduced by reading. The gap is narrower than it looks: `Subscriber.establish` reloads on the first connection and on every reconnection, and `Subscriber.Run` bounds silence with `REDIS_SUBSCRIBER_READ_TIMEOUT` and probes rather than trusting it (F30's fix), so a replica that stops hearing gets a reload when it notices. What has no backstop is a **published message that is simply lost** while the subscription stays healthy — Redis pub/sub is at-most-once — and on an instance with no Redis at all, where a second replica would never reload | **Moderate** on any multi-replica deployment, restated 2026-08-04 (M45) from *"moderate … with Redis unavailable, minor otherwise"*, which named only one of the two limbs its own evidence establishes. Both limbs are moderate and neither needs Redis to be down: a published message simply **lost** while the subscription stays healthy — pub/sub is at-most-once, and `Subscriber.establish`'s reload only catches silence it notices — and an instance with no Redis at all, where the only reload sites are boot (`cmd/linkctrl/main.go:654`) and `HostCache.Refresh` (`internal/redirect/hosts.go:164-177`), which nothing but the subscriber calls — so a second replica never reloads at all. **Minor** on a single replica, where boot and on-demand reloads cover it. It fails in the direction of continuing to serve a hostname that has been unverified, which is exactly the staleness M40 exists to bound. A ticker calling `Reload` at `DOMAIN_VERIFY_INTERVAL` would close both for a query against a table of tens of rows. Out of spec here because m40.md asks for invalidation *wired into M23's pub/sub*, which is what shipped | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 1, moderate and above. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. A `host-reload` job re-reads each replica's verified-hostname set on `DOMAIN_VERIFY_INTERVAL`. **It takes no leadership, and that is the fix rather than an oversight** — the set is in-process, so a reload the leader performs does nothing for the other replicas; it is the only job on that clock without leadership and the comment says why. Both limbs close together: a message lost on a healthy subscription is never noticed because `establish` only catches silence the replica *notices*, and an instance with no Redis has no subscriber at all. `Reload` rather than `Refresh`, because a timer has no burst to collapse and inline means a slow query delays the next tick instead of overlapping it; a failure is logged and the replica keeps the set it has. **`cmd/linkctrl` had no tests at all**, which is part of why this shipped — the claim is about a job running without leadership, and `test/integration` cannot import package main. `jobs_test.go` is the first, and `make test-integration` and the Taskfile mirror now cover the package. Sabotaged by removing the reload. Four operator-facing claims corrected: `operations.md` gains the job row (and its `mail` row stops asserting F52's false half), `configuration.md` says the interval drives both halves and what `0` now costs, and `SECURITY.md`'s *on every replica* names the two mechanisms carrying it instead of the weaker one alone. |

Fix note: adding foreign keys puts a constraint on the partitioned write path that `00400` and `00600:134` deliberately keep clean; delete the three rollups inside the existing transaction and correct the two comments | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `DeleteOrganizationRollups` takes `link_click_daily`, `link_dimension_daily` and `workspace_click_daily` for the organization's workspaces, in the same transaction and **before** the organization row goes — the workspaces are the only way to name these rows and the cascade takes the workspaces. | F67 | M36 | The per-link rule ceiling and the one-kind-per-split rule are both **read outside the transaction that writes**, so two concurrent creates can pass a check neither would pass alone | `internal/link/routing.go:144` — `CountRoutingRules` is called before `s.pool.Begin` in `CreateRule` (M34); `internal/link/split.go` — `CreateVariant` reads `CountRoutingRules` and `loadSplit` the same way, before its own `Begin` | Noticed while writing `CreateVariant`, which replicates M34's pattern deliberately so that the two writers behave identically. The window is real: two `POST /links/{id}/rules` at 20 rules each see 19, both insert, and the link ends with 21 — past the ceiling that exists because the whole list travels inside the cached snapshot and is walked on every uncached resolve. The split version is worse in kind rather than in size: two concurrent `POST /links/{id}/split`, one `weighted` and one `sequential`, both read a link with no arms, `ValidateSplitKind` passes for both, and the link ends with a **mixed split** — the exact state the validation exists to make impossible. The redirect path survives it (`Snapshot.Split` takes the first arm's kind and ignores the other), but the state should not be reachable. No `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`, no advisory lock and no unique constraint stands between them | **Minor.** Needs two writes to the same link inside a few milliseconds, which is a person double-submitting a form or a script, not ordinary use; and the worst outcome is a slightly over-long rule list or a split whose second kind is silently ignored rather than anything a visitor can observe as wrong. The fix is to move both reads inside the transaction — a `FOR UPDATE` on the link row would serialise every writer for that link — and it belongs to both milestones rather than to one, which is why it is a row rather than an in-spec fix here | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. Deliberately **not merged** with [F108](#open) or [F127](#closed) by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04. One class — a read-check-write with nothing serializing it — at three sites, but three files, three invariants, three fixes and three tiers: this row needs `FOR UPDATE` on the link row, F108 needs a transaction and a cycle predicate on the folder tree, F127 needs a row lock on an organization count and loses nothing when it races. Only F127 is approved as work. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `LockLink` takes `FOR UPDATE` on the link row inside the transaction that writes, and every guard the write depends on is decided after it: the rule ceiling in `CreateRule`, and the ceiling plus the one-kind and one-fallback rules in `CreateVariant` via a new `loadSplitWith` that reads through the transaction's handle. The link rather than the rules, because Postgres takes `FOR KEY SHARE` on a parent when a referencing row is inserted — so a locked link cannot acquire a rule or an arm, including the first one, where a lock over the rules would have had no rows to take. The outer checks stay, and are now the *message* rather than the rule: they let a caller learn about a full link beside a bad destination in one answer. Each ceiling message is one function, so the two call sites cannot drift. **Two test designs passed against the defect before one held** — racing goroutines (the window is tens of microseconds; six racers produced one winner with the fix removed) and merely holding the link row (the insert's own `FOR KEY SHARE` blocks either way). What discriminates is moving the count *under* a create already parked on the lock; removing only the re-read then writes the twenty-first rule and fails three assertions. **Coverage is honest about its edge**: the deterministic test drives `CreateRule`, and `CreateVariant` shares the mechanism rather than having a race test of its own. |
| F51 | M32.9 | `invite.Redeem` verifies a password with no per-account lockout and no failed-login record | `internal/invite/invite.go:588-601`, against `internal/auth/service.go:299-327` | The headline is a conflation and dies: the per-IP login limiter **does** cover redemption — `router.go:204` wraps it in the *same* `"login"` bucket as `/auth/login`, deliberately, so alternating cannot double the budget. Verified live: 8 wrong guesses returned 404 with `failed_login_count=0` and `locked_until=NULL`; a burst of 20 returned 429 from the sixth. What survives is narrow: redeem lacks the per-**account** lockout that login also carries, so a *distributed* attacker holding a live, address-bound, single-use 32-byte token can guess that one account's password at per-IP-limited speed without tripping the cross-source lock. D27 weighs enumeration, not brute force, so this is genuinely unweighed | **Low.** Heavily gated by possession of a scarce token, and a successful guess yields nothing beyond what the same guess at `/auth/login` yields. Worth parity — record failures and honour the lock — not urgency | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `Redeem` refuses a locked account and feeds the same counter, so the lockout an operator configured now covers both places a password is checked rather than one. The policy is built once in `main.go` and handed to both services, because two copies of a threshold are two things that can drift. `RecordFailedLogin` runs on `s.q` and not on the transaction's `q` — the refusal path returns an error and the deferred rollback would have discarded the count into nothing, which is the detail that makes this a fix rather than the appearance of one. The refusal stays `ErrNotRedeemable`, so D27's uniform answer is untouched and the lockout is not observable through redemption; a refusal does not spend the invitation. Both halves sabotaged separately — short-circuiting the lock check fails four assertions, forcing the threshold to zero fails the counter one — because a test claiming two things needs to fail for each of them. |
| F52 | M32.9 | Clearing `SMTP_HOST` on an instance that had one strands every queued message forever: the drain and the purge are gated on the same nil mailer | `cmd/linkctrl/jobs.go:189-191` and `:370-376` | Both guards confirmed. The package comment at `internal/mail/mail.go:9-11` — *"an instance with no `SMTP_HOST` … the outbox stays empty"* — is the actual defect: true only if SMTP was never configured, false across a configured-then-removed transition. The finder's *stronger half* is **not** an independent finding: `PurgeFinishedMail`'s `status <> 'pending'` is correct by design, because with a mailer no row stays pending — `Drain` claims and leases each one and transitions it to `sent` or, after 5 attempts, `failed`, and the lease recovers crash-interrupted rows. So the whole finding reduces to the SMTP-removal transition | **Low.** The relay is gone, so nothing deliverable is lost; the result is orphaned invisible rows (`Service.Pending` has no non-test caller) bounded by what was enqueued before removal, produced only by a deliberate uncommon operator action. Best fixed as an invariant correction or a one-time drain-then-purge on teardown | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `AbandonUnsendableMail` fails pending rows past the 30-day window on the no-mailer path, and the purge job runs it in the `else` the mailer guard never had. Failed rather than deleted, so the row leaves by the same retention path as every other finished message instead of by a second delete, and its body goes for `MarkMailFailed`'s reason. Past the window rather than at once, so an operator who clears `SMTP_HOST` by mistake and restores it the same afternoon still gets their queue delivered. The finder's *stronger half* stays refuted and nothing was built for it. Two comments that asserted the false half — `internal/mail/mail.go`'s *the outbox stays empty* and `jobs.go`'s *with no mailer nothing is ever enqueued* — now say what is true across the configured-then-removed transition, and `docs/configuration.md` tells an operator what turning the relay off does to what is already queued. Sabotaged by inverting the window; it abandons both rows and fails. |
| F11 | M28 | `TestInvalidationReachesAnotherReplica` is flaky | `test/integration/invalidation_test.go:276` — "the second replica did not cache the alias in process" | Observed 2026-08-01 while running M28's gates. Two consecutive uncached full runs (`GOFLAGS=-count=1 make test-integration`) of the same tree: the first failed on this assertion, the second passed, and three earlier full runs of the same tree during the milestone passed. Nothing in M28 touches the redirect path, the resolver, Redis or the invalidation subscriber — the milestone's diff is `internal/team`, `internal/httpx`, `internal/ui`, `internal/auth`'s provisioning helper and one migration — so the change under test is not a plausible cause. The assertion is that a *second* replica has populated its in-process tier, which is reached by a prior request whose caching is asynchronous relative to the assertion; that is the shape a timing-dependent assertion has. | Minor, and test-only — no product behaviour is implicated. The cost is a gate that fails for the wrong reason, which is expensive in a loop that treats a failing gate as a stop condition: a flake here reads identically to a real regression in whatever milestone happens to be in flight, and the next person will spend the same run re-running it to find out which. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 5, minor — one-edit corrections. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. The test warms the second replica's cache without waiting for the subscriber it started in a goroutine, and every establishment — including the first — flushes both in-process tiers, which is D20 and is correct. So the warm-up was racing a flush it had no way to see, which is why one run in five failed on a tree that never touched the redirect path. It now waits on `PUBSUB NUMSUB` for the channel before warming: Redis is asked, because there is no readiness hook and a test-only row is not where one gets added to the product. The barrier also makes the invalidation half prove itself — without it the publish could beat the subscription, the message be missed, and the establishment flush drop the entry anyway, so the test passed while demonstrating nothing about the thing it exists for. Eight consecutive runs green; sabotaged by publishing to a dead channel, which times it out. |
| F63 | M28 | `CreateWorkspace` gates on the identity's `workspace.write`, so a **workspace-scoped** admin adds workspaces to the whole organization — the one member-adjacent write M28's reopening left on the identity's union rather than on the membership that carried the permission (D44) | `internal/team/workspace.go:88` — `if !actor.Can(PermWorkspaceWrite)`, against `RenameWorkspace` and `DeleteWorkspace` immediately below it, which both resolve the permission against the *target* workspace through `writableWorkspace` | Read against the tree while building D44's bound, and the function's own doc comment states the mismatch: *"the authority being exercised is over the organization the caller is currently in"* — which is exactly the authority a workspace-scoped membership does not carry. `internal/store/query/members.sql` says a workspace-scoped membership grants authority over one workspace, and `00700_seed.sql` grants `workspace.write` to `owner` and `admin`, so any workspace-scoped admin reaches it. Not reproduced end to end: the D44 tests added in M28 cover the membership writes and stop where this begins | **Low.** It creates rather than reads or destroys, the new workspace is one the actor holds no membership in and therefore cannot act in, and no existing data is reachable through it — the cost is organization-wide clutter an owner must clean up, not access. Worth closing for consistency: after D44 it is the one write in the package that answers the old question | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `CreateWorkspace` takes organization-wide `workspace.write` through `LoadMembershipAuthority(...).In(nil)` — D44's shape, and the same one `invite.orgWideAuthority` uses — instead of the identity's union. `Can` is still checked first, so somebody holding the permission nowhere gets the plain refusal rather than the scope one. The absence of a target workspace is what made the old gate look right; what settles it is that the *object created* belongs to the organization. The test refuses from an identity that legitimately holds `workspace.write` in its own workspace, because one holding nothing would have passed against the old gate and proved nothing; sabotaged by short-circuiting the new check, which fails it on both the error and the row count. `docs/SECURITY.md`'s enumeration of what only an organization-wide membership reaches gained *a new workspace* — the claim was already there and this row is what had made it false. |

A workspace under a soft-deleted organization could be resolved into but never listed, so the switcher would mark nothing selected and a browser would show the first entry while the session acted somewhere else. **Latent today**: `DeleteOrganization` (`organizations.sql:71`) is a hard `DELETE` and nothing in the tree ever sets `organizations.deleted_at`, so the two queries agree in practice. It becomes live the moment anything soft-deletes an organization | Minor, latent. Filed because the asymmetry is invisible from either query alone | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `ResolveWorkspaceForUser` joins `organizations` and filters `o.deleted_at IS NULL`, which is what `ListWorkspacesForUser` has always done — so the two statements of one scoping rule now agree on purpose rather than by accident. Fixed while latent and deliberately so: `DeleteOrganization` is a hard `DELETE` and nothing in the tree writes `organizations.deleted_at`, so nothing could provoke it, and the asymmetry is invisible from either query alone — which is why it survived to be found by review. `TestASoftDeletedOrganizationIsNeitherListedNorResolvedInto` sets the column directly, pins the resolve *and* the list halves beside each other, and was sabotaged: dropping the filter fails it on both assertions. |

The theme scan reads `funcs.go` as well as the template embed — the one file `input.css` names explicitly as a class source, and the one file the guard could not see. Verified by performing the row's own sabotage: rewriting the status-badge map to raw emerald shades now fails by line and utility, where before it left both theme tests green while Tailwind emitted the classes and four template sites rendered them. Listed rather than walked, so the scanned set and the stylesheet's `@source` lines stay the same set. **The [F121](#closed) limb is closed by refutation, not by code**: the six hand-written redirect-tree pages stay unscanned, because m24.5.md scopes the override to *every **dashboard** page* and already exempts a non-dashboard surface, these are embedded byte slices with no template, and since M40 that tree answers on hostnames the theme cookie never reaches. Recorded in m24.5.md's reopening rather than dropped. |

| F102 | M44.9 | For the three Redis-shared limiters, the tracked-keys gauge is pinned at zero while the feature works, and the runbook's alert for a degraded shared limit has no expression that can fire | `internal/ratelimit/ratelimit.go:213-218` returns before the local shard write and before the overflow counter; `internal/observability/collectors.go:153-159` declares only `Len()` and `Overflows()`; `internal/ratelimit/shared.go:221` (`Fallbacks`) is referenced only by tests; `docs/operations.md:116` | On a healthy Redis the local table for `login`, `api` and `link_password` is never written, so `linkctrl_rate_limit_tracked_keys{limit="login"}` reads 0 — indistinguishable from no traffic — and the gauge's documented meaning at `collectors.go:154` is false. `operations.md:116` prescribes watching a rate that stays *"unchanged"*, which is not an expression. Narrowed by verification: a fallback **does** write the local table, so a non-zero reading is a working, undocumented fallback detector, and the runbook row does also point at a log line | Minor to moderate, observability only; fail-open is the accepted posture. The cost is that an operator following the shipped runbook cannot tell a shared limit from a fallen-back one, on the two limiters whose entire justification for a Redis round trip is that per-replica multiplication is unacceptable. Fix note: writing the local bucket on shared success is explicitly rejected at `ratelimit.go:208-212`, because a permanently empty bucket destroys failover; and `Fallbacks()` is monotonic, so a naive gauge alert latches forever after one transient blip | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 2, minor to moderate. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. A new counter, `linkctrl_rate_limit_fallback_total`, and a runbook row that is an expression. The tracked-keys gauge could not answer this and the row leaves it alone: a healthy shared limiter never writes its local table, so `Len()` reads zero and is indistinguishable from no traffic. The two fixes the row's own note rules out are both avoided — writing the local bucket on shared success is rejected at `ratelimit.go` because a permanently empty bucket destroys failover, and a threshold on `Fallbacks()` latches forever because it is monotonic, which is why this is a **counter** the alert reads as a rate. `docs/operations.md` said to watch a rate that *"stays unchanged"*, which is a description rather than an expression; it now carries one. |
| F109 | M44.9 | A recipient's email address reaches the process log unredacted, on the first bounce | `internal/mail/mail.go:181` appends `sendErr` to the returned error on **every** attempt, which `cmd/linkctrl/jobs.go:260` logs at ERROR as `slog.Any("error", err)`; also `mail.go:189-193`. `internal/mail/smtp.go:113` wraps the relay's response line, which routinely echoes the address | `internal/observability/logging.go:59-63` redacts by attribute **name** and `error` is not in the list, so `550 5.1.1 <alice@example.com>: User unknown` passes through. No `slog` attribute anywhere else in the tree carries an address, so this defeats a convention the package otherwise keeps — and `internal/webhook/client.go:288-296` unwraps `*url.Error` for exactly this reason one package over. Reachability is higher than first reported: it needs one bounce on the first attempt, not exhaustion of the retry budget. `mail_outbox.last_error` is **not** part of this — `recipient` is already a column on that row | Minor, and it needs SMTP configured. It moves an address from the database, which is access-controlled and retention-bounded, into the log stream, which is typically shipped elsewhere with no LinkCtrl-side bound. Same mechanism as F34 in a second package | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `scrubAddress` replaces the recipient wherever a relay echoed it, before the error reaches `slog`. Replaced rather than dropped: the status code and the relay's wording are the operational content, and an error reduced to *delivery failed* trades a disclosure for an outage nobody can diagnose. Case-insensitive because SMTP is, and the local part alone is covered too, since a relay may answer *mailbox alice does not exist*. **`mail_outbox.last_error` is deliberately untouched** — that row already carries `recipient` as a column and is access-controlled and retention-bounded, which is exactly the distinction the row draws. Reachability was higher than first reported: one bounce on the first attempt, not exhaustion of the retry budget. |
| F59 | M32.9 | Two narrow privacy truncation gaps: `/48` preserves the entire client IPv4 for 6to4 addresses, and the in-memory salt cache retains salts past the database deletion described as the de-identification step | `internal/auth/session.go:167-186`; `internal/analytics/salt.go:86-96` and `:138-153` | Arithmetic reproduced: `2002:cb00:712a::1` → `2002:cb00:712a::/48`, whose bytes 2–5 are `203.0.113.42`. Every other v4-in-v6 scheme was tested and is safe — Teredo, NAT64 and ISATAP all embed in the low bits that `/48` discards, and `::ffff:` is folded explicitly with a comment naming the bug class. 6to4 is a single blind spot against a population RFC 7526 deprecated in 2015. The salt half was corrected in the **worse** direction: `Purge` evicts to `today−2` in the same call that deleted `today−2`'s row, so the leader is affected too and it reproduces on the single-replica default; and the eviction inside `For` runs only on a cache miss, so a replica that stops receiving clicks never trims. But the category is over-read: `README.md:126` and `salt.go:135` are claims about what a **database** holds — an erasure request, a backup, a dump — and a heap copy in a live process is none of those | **Trivial**, both. Filed together because both are one-line corrections on the privacy boundary that cost nothing to take, and because the salt one is the kind of off-by-one that makes a proud sentence not quite true. Neither is at risk from any attacker who does not already have more | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 8, cosmetic and trivial. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Both one-line corrections on the privacy boundary, and both checked rather than assumed. **6to4** folds to its embedded IPv4 before masking: `2002::/16` carries the client's address in bytes 2-5, inside the `/48` an IPv6 address is kept at, so `2002:cb00:712a::1` masked to `2002:cb00:712a::/48` — `203.0.113.42` in full. Every other v4-in-v6 scheme was tested and needs nothing: Teredo, NAT64 and ISATAP all embed in the low bits a `/48` discards, and each is now a test case, because a fold applied too widely would turn a real IPv6 client into somebody else's IPv4 address. **The salt cache's predicate is now the SQL's**, not an approximation: a salt for day D carries `purge_at = D + 2` and the statement deletes `purge_at < now()`, so D is gone the moment D+2 begins — while the cache evicted `d.Before(today-2)`, which keeps exactly that day. The process held the one salt the de-identification step had just deleted. Eviction also runs on a cache **hit** now, and Purge trims unconditionally: the other evictor runs under leadership, so on a follower a hit was the only eviction there was. |
| F118 | M44.9 | Teardown writes alias reservations that the same statement's cascade deletes, for links on an organization- or workspace-owned domain | `internal/store/migrations/00300_links.sql:174` keys `reserved_aliases` to `domain_id` with `ON DELETE CASCADE`; `domains.organization_id` (`00300:12`) and `domains.workspace_id` (`02500:29`) both cascade from their parents; against `internal/team/organization.go:305` and `internal/team/workspace.go:243` | The reservation inserted immediately before the delete is removed by the cascade of that delete. Severity corrected sharply downward by verification: F28's exposure is the **shared default domain**, whose `organization_id` is NULL and which is untouched — `organizations.sql:104-107` says so and both regression tests use it — so the path that matters is intact. For a custom hostname the domain row is destroyed too, and re-serving that alias would require re-registering the hostname *and* passing M40's TXT check, at which point whoever does that controls the name anyway | Cosmetic. Self-defeating bookkeeping rather than self-defeating security: wasted inserts on every teardown that owns a custom domain, and an overstated sentence at `organization.go:216-225`. Fix note: `RESTRICT` makes organization deletion fail outright, `SET NULL` is impossible because `domain_id` is half the primary key, and re-keying reservations by hostname would reserve aliases on a name whose next owner proved control of it | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 8, cosmetic and trivial. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Recorded at `DeleteOrganization`, where the overstated sentence was. `reserved_aliases` is keyed to `domain_id` with `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so for a link on a workspace's own hostname the reservation this teardown inserts is removed by the cascade of the same statement — wasted inserts rather than a wrong outcome. **Nothing is done about it and the comment now carries every reason**: F28's exposure is the shared default domain, whose `organization_id` is NULL and which teardown does not touch, so the path that matters is intact; re-serving a custom hostname's alias needs re-registering the name and passing the TXT check, at which point whoever did that controls it anyway; and each repair is worse — `RESTRICT` makes deletion fail outright, `SET NULL` is impossible because `domain_id` is half the primary key, and re-keying by hostname would reserve aliases on a name whose next owner proved control of it. |
| F120 | M44.9 | Both `Roles` functions filter on the identity's union rank, so a control offers a role the service then refuses — on the rank axis to a browser, and on the credential axis to an API key | `internal/team/team.go:254` and `internal/invite/invite.go:485` both filter on `actor.RoleRank`, while the ceilings read `here.Rank` (`team.go:211`) and `orgWide.Rank` (`invite.go:302`); `invite.Roles` is rendered by `internal/httpx/web_invitations.go:55`, and D43's API-key cap sits at `invite.go:302` inside `Create`, which both `POST /invites` and `POST /api/v1/invitations` reach | An organization-wide viewer who also holds a workspace-scoped admin row has a union rank of the stronger membership, so `Roles` lists admin while `invite.Create` compares against the organization-wide rank and refuses. `invite.go:296-301` names the exact hazard it did not fix. Both doc comments claim the opposite — *"a control cannot offer something `resolveRole` will then refuse"*. **Folded in from F62 (M27), 2026-08-04**, which found the same absent mirror on the other axis and, being older, carried anchors that had moved (`invite.go:450` → `:485`, the cap `:293` → `:302`). `Roles` mirrors D28's rank ceiling and not D43's cap, so a caller authenticated with an **API key** is offered `owner` and `admin` and gets a `403` on submit. A key reaches that page because `signedIn` (`internal/httpx/router.go:286`) is `RequireWebAuth`+`RequireOrganization`, `RequireWebAuth` (`internal/httpx/web.go:290`) tests only that an identity exists, and `BearerAuth` is applied outside `Session` (`router.go:413`) so a bearer token attaches one. It was raised at M27's step 3.4 as the worker's stated reason for *not* filtering `Roles` — *"the role form is signedIn-only so no key can reach it"* — and that reason is false. Bounded, and the bound is why it was never in spec: both routes call `invite.Service.Create`, where the cap sits, so the only reachable outcome is a refusal at submit. | Cosmetic. Nothing is reachable that the service permits; both ceilings hold and D44 is not weakened. The cost is a dropdown that 403s and three comments asserting it cannot — `invite.go:473` claims *"a control cannot offer something `resolveRole` will then refuse"*, and `Roles`'s own doc points at **the ceiling check** as the thing it applies, which is exactly what this row falsifies. F62 was filed at **Trivial** on the strength of that claim and is folded in here rather than triaged against it. `web_keys.go:55` is the same shape done the other way — a UI filter mirroring `NonDelegableScopes` — which is what makes the absent mirror here worth one row rather than none | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 8, cosmetic and trivial. **[F62](#closed) (M27) was merged into this row** by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04: two rows against the same two functions, one of them resting its severity on a premise the other falsifies. One filter change answers both axes. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. **The false sentence is corrected and the filter is deliberately not narrowed**, which is the opposite of what the row's title suggests and is what working it established. `invite.Roles` was already right — it reads the organization-wide rank, exactly as `Create`'s ceiling does, and its comment explains why — so that axis had closed. `team.Roles` is the one whose claim was false: it said *a control cannot offer something resolveRole will then refuse*, while `resolveRole` compares against the authority covering the **target**, and this one list is rendered for a page whose rows target different workspaces and whose grant form chooses its target on submit. Narrowing to the organization-wide rank is right for invitations and **wrong here**: an invitation admits somebody to the whole organization, one ceiling governs it; a role assignment is per membership, and the weakest authority would hide admin from the workspace-scoped admin who may genuinely grant it. No single list is exact for many targets, so the comment now says the service is where exactness lives. D43's cap stays applied, because it is absolute rather than per-target. Absorbs F62. |
| F123 | M44.9 | The shared limiter's circuit breaker has no half-open state, so every concurrent request retries when the cooldown lapses | `internal/ratelimit/shared.go:250-257` — `allow()` reads under the mutex, sets nothing, and marks no probe in flight; `openUntil` is re-armed only in `recordFailure` at `:267`, after the failed call returns | The comment at `:228-233` promises *"an outage costs the timeout a few times and then nothing until the cooldown lets **one** request through to check"*, and `:61-62` repeats it. Severity corrected down by verification: the cost is `REDIS_READ_TIMEOUT` (50ms by default) once every five-second cooldown, a one per cent duty cycle rather than a stall, and it only becomes the herd the finding describes if an operator raises that timeout | Cosmetic to minor at defaults, moderate at a retuned read timeout. The comment is what the next person would trust when deciding whether the breaker needs work. Fix note: a compare-and-swap half-open must re-arm `openUntil` when the probe is **dispatched**, not on its result, or the probe's own timeout window is an open door; and a failed probe must re-open immediately rather than waiting for the failure threshold again. **Tier restated 2026-08-04 (M45) and held.** The range already reflects the widening: verification is what corrected it down, and the tier is stated as a function of the one setting that moves it, `REDIS_READ_TIMEOUT`. Cosmetic at the shipped 50ms, where the cost is a one per cent duty cycle; moderate wherever an operator has raised it, which is a documented thing to do. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 8, cosmetic and trivial. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. A real half-open, built the way the row's fix note required: `allow()` **re-arms `openUntil` when the probe is dispatched**, not when it answers, so exactly one caller per cooldown reaches Redis and the rest are refused without paying the timeout. Deferring the re-arm to the result would leave the probe's own timeout window open to the same herd, which is the trap the note names. A failed probe needs no special case — the breaker is already armed, so it stays shut for another cooldown without having to reach the failure threshold again — and a successful one clears it in `succeed()`. Shown red by restoring the read-without-write: twenty of twenty requests admitted. The tier the row carried was Cosmetic at the shipped 50ms and moderate at a retuned `REDIS_READ_TIMEOUT`, which is a documented thing to do; the comment that promised this behaviour is now true at both. |

A links surface that visibly fails to follow a switch, where falling back to `/links` would have worked | Minor. One dead end, recoverable by navigating; no data is exposed or lost | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `switchTarget` collapses a link's detail page to `/links` in the switcher's `next`, and leaves every other path alone. The path named an id belonging to the workspace being left, so following it rendered Not found — a control that visibly fails to follow a switch, on the one surface where the collection view would have worked. To `/links` rather than `/dashboard`, because the reader was looking at links and still wants links. The table test asserts the eight paths that must stay put alongside the two that must not, since a rule that trimmed more would throw away the page a reader chose. |
| F42 | M32.9 | `Dispute.Liftable` conflates "a blocklist row caused this" with "an allow can succeed", so the review queue draws an **Allow** button on exactly the disputes the code already knows will 409 | `internal/dispute/dispute.go:236` (`liftableRules` admits `operator_blocklist`), `:545` (`entryToLift` refuses `SourceEnv`), `:712`, and `internal/ui/templates/pages/disputes.html:82-89` | Reproduced: `SeedBlocklist(["envblocked.example"])`, judge, file, Allow → `conflict: "envblocked.example" comes from LINKCTRL_DESTINATION_BLOCKLIST …`, dispute still `open`. A source census after a boot seed on a fully migrated database is `map[env:2 shortener:19]` — **no `review` row exists or can exist**, because the only runtime insert hardcodes `'env'` and the seed hardcodes `'shortener'`. The template renders the Allow form on `{{if .Liftable}}` and renders the *"nothing to remove"* warning only when `not .Liftable`, so an env-sourced dispute gets the button and not the warning. The 409 itself is deliberate, tested (`dispute_test.go:487`) and documented — it is the button that is wrong. M30's tier table is **not** falsified: a shortener row lifts cleanly, verified. Related and folded in: `01500:45` says `'review'` is *"what M31's owner review **will** write"* and `created_by` is written by no statement in the tree, which is the mechanical reason nobody noticed | **Minor to moderate.** Contradicts `dispute.go:140-145` — *"The page draws its buttons from this rather than making the owner discover it by clicking"* — for the most likely dispute an operator-configured instance will see, and `webError` turns the 409 into an error page rather than in-context guidance. Same family as F21: a control that looks like it does something and does not | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 2, minor to moderate. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `Liftable` now asks both questions: whether the *rule* is list-backed, and whether the **entry behind this refusal** is one a decision may delete. The queue's two queries carry the entry's source through a LEFT JOIN, and `canLiftEntry` is spelled once and consulted by the flag the page draws from — so the button and the refusal cannot disagree, which is what this was: `entryToLift` refused an `env`-sourced entry and `Liftable` never asked. A source census on a migrated database returns only `env` and `shortener`, so this was not an edge case but the most likely dispute on any operator-configured instance. Both paths that return a freshly written row resolve the source too — `File` with one lookup, `decide` from the row it already read **before** the decision, since an allow has just deleted the entry and reporting *not liftable* because the lift succeeded would be the opposite of informative. Shown red by restoring the rule-only flag. |
| F47 | M32.9 | For an account that belongs to nothing, the notification bell's **View all** link is a dead end — `/notifications` sits behind `RequireOrganization` and 303s straight back | `internal/ui/templates/partials/nav.html:224`, against `internal/httpx/router.go:344` + `:287` and `internal/httpx/web_team.go:469` | Rendered the orphan page rather than reasoning about it: the only href not gated on anything is `/notifications`. `layout.html:33` invokes the bell unconditionally while the identity menu beside it gates Account and Feeds on `HasOrganization`. The finder's premise that organization deletion is soft is **false** (`organizations.sql:71-83` is a hard delete that cascades) — the conclusion survives for a better reason: `notifications.workspace_id` is nullable and all four producers leave it nil, so the workspaces cascade never reaches a notification row, and the count/preview queries are user-scoped. The orphan is also the *likeliest* holder: deleting an organization needs the owner role, and owners are the recipient class for audit-growth, invite-accepted and dispute-filed. `TestTheHeaderOffersNothingToAnAccountThatBelongsToNothing` cuts the nav *before* the bell, so despite its name it cannot see this | **Minor.** No authorization consequence — `RequireOrganization` is explicitly *"an affordance and not the authorization boundary"*. Its **View all** was the only href in the header not gated on anything, and `/notifications` sits behind `RequireOrganization` and 303s straight back — offered to exactly the account most likely to hold unread notifications, since deleting an organization needs the owner role and owners are the recipient class for audit growth, invitations and disputes. The renderer refuses a template referencing a key nobody passed, which is what caught the new branch immediately, and the fixture now renders **both** sides — a template with an `if` exercised on one side has an untested half, and the untested half is the one that only appears on somebody else's instance. |

The front door says what is true. README's status block read *Phase 1 complete, released as 0.1.0* on a page listing a dozen Phase 2 features in the present tense and pointing at a changelog section that states their absence; it now says **Phase 2 complete and unreleased, 0.1.0 is the latest tag**, and names what pinning that tag actually gets.
