In Plan.md: the *as of 2026-07-31, Phase 2 is planned and unstarted* snapshot is replaced by a statement with **no counts in it**, because status lives in phase-details/README.md and a second copy is what went stale; *one open finding, cosmetic, unreviewed* likewise stops repeating a number the deferred-findings file owns, against a queue that had passed sixty and been triaged three times; *five insertions* becomes six, which is what the same sentence listed; and *Deliberately accepted in Phase 1* over the limitations table becomes accurate, since most of those rows rest on Phase 2 decisions. **The `Since 0.2.0` phrasings are left alone**: they describe the version this milestone is preparing, and what made them misleading was a status line claiming 0.1.0 was current — which is the thing that changed. The status line moves to a released 0.2.0 at the tag, which is this milestone's own release step. |

| F64 | M33 | `ServeMux`'s own path-cleaning redirect is the one response on the redirect tree that carries none of the tree's headers — no `nosniff`, no `X-Robots-Tag`, no `Cache-Control` — and it serves an HTML body | `net/http`, `ServeMux.findHandler`; the rule it sits outside is stated at `internal/httpx/redirect.go`, `errorPage` — *"the redirect tree is outside the security-header chain, so every response it writes sets nosniff itself"* | Reproduced on the built image: `GET /ld1//deep` answers `307 Temporary Redirect`, `Content-Type: text/html; charset=utf-8`, `Location: /ld1/deep`, 45 bytes of HTML, and **none** of the three headers every other response on that tree sets. Same for `/ld1/../ld2` → `/ld2`. `ServeMux` cleans the escaped path before dispatching and answers the redirect itself, so no handler in this repository is involved and nothing the project writes can add headers to it. **Pre-existing rather than caused by M33** — `cleanPath` applies to every request whatever patterns are registered, so `/foo/../bar` behaved this way before the deep-link route existed. What M33 changes is how many paths reach it and end somewhere real: before, a cleaned two-segment path redirected and then 404ed. Verified **not** a 301, which would have broken the absolute *redirects are 302, never 301* rule: it is 307. The body is Go-escaped, so the reflected path is not an injection vector | **Minor.** No alias or destination is disclosed that the request did not already contain, and the missing header with any bite — `nosniff` on a `text/html` body — protects against sniffing content that is already HTML and already escaped. The cost is that the tree's stated invariant is not actually true of every response it produces, and the honest fix is either to wrap the mux so the headers are set on the way out or to amend the claim | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 4, minor — redirect path and cache. The batch owes one k6 re-run, not six. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `alwaysNosniff` wraps every mux that can serve an alias — the split-host link mux, the custom-hostname mux, and the single-host mux — so `X-Content-Type-Options` is on the one response no handler in this repository writes: `ServeMux`'s own path-cleaning 307, which carries a `text/html` body. **Only nosniff, and that is a choice with a reason.** It is the missing header with any bite on an HTML body, and it is already set by every handler here and by the application tree's middleware, so applying it everywhere changes no response this project writes. `X-Robots-Tag` and `Cache-Control: no-store` are *error-page* policy rather than tree policy — `Location` deliberately sets a different `Cache-Control` and no robots header, because a short link is meant to be shared — so setting those in the wrapper would have changed successful redirects in order to fix a 307. The comment in `redirect.go` claiming the invariant now says what it covers. Shown red by removing the wrapper's one line. |
| F100 | M44.9 | `HEAD` advances a sequential split's durable rotation, re-phasing every subsequent visitor's arm with no click recorded | `internal/httpx/redirect_split.go:53` and `:127-136` never test `r.Method`, while `internal/httpx/redirect.go:454` (`record`) and `internal/httpx/redirect_gates.go:229-235` (budget) both do | Falsifies two absolutes in one decision entry — `decisions.md:9225-9231`, *"**The budget is the only gate that consumes something**"* and *"HEAD never consumes at all"* — because M36 put a second durable counter in the same table. A link checker or unfurler shifts the rotation on every probe, and because no click row is written the per-destination breakdown cannot show why the arms are uneven. The companion claim, that a deep link the alias cannot forward also spends its position, is **not** a finding: D57 records that deliberately at `redirect_split.go:114-121`, `decisions.md:9406-9413` and `Plan.md:810` | Minor. Skews a split test under crawler traffic; no visitor sees a wrong page and nothing is destroyed. Fix note: returning early on HEAD makes a HEAD answer the link's *own* destination while a GET answers an arm, so a checker validates a URL no visitor is sent to — HEAD must still choose an arm without advancing, which `NextVariantRotation` cannot do | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 3, minor — a principal can act on it, in scope by the 2026-08-04 standing rule. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `PeekVariantRotation` and `gate.PeekRotation`: a HEAD chooses the arm the next GET would get and does not advance the durable counter. The row's own fix note ruled out the obvious repair and it was right — returning early on HEAD would have a checker validate the link's *own* destination, a URL no visitor is ever sent to — so HEAD had to keep choosing, which `NextVariantRotation` could not do. The read-only twin is **Budget's shape exactly**, for the same caller and the same reason: HEAD must be answered with what a visitor would get and must not change it. No row means position 1, which is the first arm. The two absolutes this row convicted in D8's entry are now true again. Shown red by taking the HEAD branch out: five probes moved the rotation from 0 to 5. |
| F115 | M44.9 | D54's per-alias password limiter has an availability cost nobody recorded, and a correct password spends a token | `internal/httpx/redirect_gates.go:161` calls `passwordAllowed` before `ParseForm` and before verification; `:204-216` keys the second limb on the alias with both limbs consumed on refusal; `LINK_PASSWORD_RATE_LIMIT` defaults to 20 refilling one per three seconds | The keying itself is D54's recorded choice and arguing it is re-litigation — the decision reasons explicitly that *"an attacker driving attempts through many visitors' browsers spreads them across addresses and walks past a per-address limit"*, and consuming both limbs on refusal is documented in the function's own doc. What is **not** recorded: D54 states the fail-open cost and no other, so nothing anywhere says a stranger can hold a password link's bucket empty and lock out its intended audience at roughly one request every three seconds. And verification found a limb nobody filed — because the limiter runs before the form is parsed, a link with more than twenty legitimate visitors in a burst throttles itself with no attacker present | Minor as a defect, since the mechanism was chosen deliberately; the row is for the missing risk note against D54 and the self-throttling limb. Fix note: dropping the alias limb or keying it on address-plus-alias reopens the distributed-guessing hole D54 exists to close, and D53's CSRF waiver is load-bearing on D54 — so any change here reopens D53. Refunding the alias token on a **correct** password touches neither decision | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 3, minor — a principal can act on it, in scope by the 2026-08-04 standing rule. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. **The self-throttling limb is fixed and the other one is recorded**, which is the split the row itself draws. A correct password refunds the per-alias token — `Limiter.RefundKey`, with the shared script clamping to burst so a negative cost cannot inflate a bucket — and that touches neither D53 nor D54, because the keying that closes distributed guessing is unchanged, an attacker without the password never reaches the refund, and what is returned was spent by somebody who proved they had it. The **address** limb stays spent deliberately: a correct password is still traffic from that address. The measurement had to be taken on the alias bucket directly, because a fixture has one address and the per-address limb refuses first — which is itself the reason the defect is invisible from a single client. The limb this does **not** close is D54's accepted cost, now written against the decision rather than left to be discovered: a stranger can hold the bucket empty with wrong guesses at roughly one request every three seconds. |
| F116 | M44.9 | With `forward_path` on, the deep-link remainder is visitor-controlled, unbounded to 1 MiB, and walked or copied several times per request | `internal/httpx/redirect.go:721-761`; `internal/platform/httpserver/server.go:43-56` sets `Addr`, `Handler`, `ReadHeaderTimeout`, `WriteTimeout` and `ErrorLog` and never `MaxHeaderBytes` | No length check exists between the router and the joiner. `joinable` walks every `/`-separated segment calling `url.PathUnescape`, then the concatenation, the unescape of the join and `u.String()` each copy the whole remainder — roughly five passes over up to a megabyte, on a request that touches no database. A cache hit is never charged to the 404-probe limiter, and `docs/slo.md:913-917` records that as deliberate | Minor, corrected down from the tier it was filed at: it is CPU and allocation amplification rather than disclosure or corruption, it needs `forward_path` switched on per link, and 1 MiB is a real ceiling rather than none. Fix note: capping total length alone misses it, because `joinable`'s per-segment unescape is driven by segment **count** — a 40 KiB path of 20,000 empty segments is 20,000 allocations. Bound both, and refuse through the existing not-forwardable 404 rather than inventing a 414 on a tree whose header invariants F64 already shows are fragile | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 3, minor — a principal can act on it, in scope by the 2026-08-04 standing rule. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. `MaxForwardedPath` (4096) and `MaxForwardedSegments` (64), checked in `joinable`. **Both, because either alone misses it** — the row's own fix note, and it is right: length alone leaves the per-segment cost, which is driven by segment *count*, so a 40 KiB path of twenty thousand empty segments is twenty thousand `PathUnescape` calls under any sane length cap. Refused through the existing not-forwardable 404 rather than a 414, as the row asked, because a new status on this tree would tell a prober something the 404 does not — and the tree's header invariants were fragile enough that F64 was open against them at the time. Generous on purpose: an amplification bound, not a validation rule. |
| F50 | M32.9 | With bot blocking on, a blocked bot writes a click event for **expired and archived** links, which recorded nothing before M32.5 | `internal/httpx/redirect.go:188-198` (the gate calls `h.record`) against `:208-216` (`OutcomeGone` returns without recording) | Reproduced both directions on one link pair. Blocking off: expired 410, archived 404, `clicks=0`, `click_count=0`. Blocking on: both 403, `expired clicks=6 archived clicks=3`, and `click_count` moved to match. Analytics are unaffected — every recorded row carries `is_bot`, and `link_click_daily` non-bot clicks stayed 0 — but `links.click_count` is rendered raw at `links_table.html:29` and `dashboard.html:45` and is the `sort=clicks` key, so an archived link can accrue a visible count from crawler traffic and outrank active links. No milestone claim is falsified: m32.5.md:64 states *"A blocked attempt is counted, not audited"* unconditionally. The direction is safe — `click_count > 0` gates both `ReserveAlias` and `PurgeExpiredLinks`, so both alias-protection paths become more protective | **Minor.** An unstated consequence of a stated rule rather than a contradiction. Identical traffic is recorded or not depending on a setting about *responses*; the honest fix is a decision — record for every state or none — not a correction. F24's family, extended to links that previously counted nothing | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 6, minor — wrong behaviour nothing can provoke. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05, [D101](../../Plan.md#phase-2-decisions-taken-after-the-plan-was-finalised), owner-answered. Recorded **in every state** rather than in none: `OutcomeGone` now records, and so does `OutcomeNotFound` when a real link is behind it — a link that exists and was asked for a path it cannot forward — while an alias that does not exist still records nothing, because there is no link to attribute a click to. The row is right that this was a decision and not a correction: no milestone claim was falsified, since m32.5.md says *"a blocked attempt is counted, not audited"* without qualification, and that sentence is untouched. *Record for none* was rejected with a reason — it makes the redirect path decide what to record from the response it was about to send, which is coupling the current shape avoids, and leaves a blocked bot on a live link counted, so the rule gains an exception instead of losing one. `TestAnExpiredLinkRecordsItsTrafficWithOrWithoutBotBlocking` puts two expired links side by side, one blocking and one not, and asserts both record; shown red by removing the call. **What this deliberately does not fix** is the surprise the row opens with: archived links go on accruing visible counts from crawlers, because `links.click_count` is rendered raw beside a rollup that counts only humans — that is [F24](#open), approved separately. |
| F105 | M44.9 | `notifications.workspace_id` is written on every M40 and M43 notification and read by nothing, while two comments state it produces a per-workspace inbox | `internal/store/query/notifications.sql:16`, `:31` and `:52` all key on `user_id` alone; `internal/notify/notify.go:483-501` (`toNotification`) never copies the column and `Notification` at `:67-78` has no field for it | `internal/notify/domains.go:45-47` and `internal/notify/automation.go:32-33` both assert, verbatim, that *"The notification carries the owning workspace so it appears in that workspace's inbox rather than wherever the reader happens to be standing."* No read path consults it, so it cannot reach any surface. `internal/notify/automation.go` also writes a body reading *"matched … in **this workspace**"* that the reader cannot resolve | Minor. Nothing leaks and nothing fails; the cost is a column written on every notification with no reader, and two comments that would stop the next person adding the filter. It is also the stated mitigation for F94 — its authors believed cross-workspace notifications were contained by an inbox scope that does not exist. Fix note: a naive `AND workspace_id = @ws` hides every organization-level notification, since disputes and audit-growth write NULL; the predicate must be `IS NULL OR = @ws`, and it must be added identically to the count and the preview or `notifications_user_unread_idx` stops serving them, which `notifications.sql:26-28` warns about | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 5, minor — one-edit corrections. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05, [D102](../../Plan.md#phase-2-decisions-taken-after-the-plan-was-finalised), owner-answered. The filter the two comments had been describing since M40 is built: `workspace_id IS NULL OR workspace_id = @workspace_id`, added **identically** to `ListNotifications`, `CountUnreadNotifications` and `ListUnreadNotificationPreview`, so `notifications_user_unread_idx` keeps serving the count and the preview and the badge cannot disagree with the page it previews. `Notification` gains the field and `toNotification` copies it; `api/openapi.yaml` declares it. Both comments now say when they became true and that they were not before. **Built rather than deleted because of [F94](#closed)**, which was closed believing cross-workspace notifications were contained by an inbox scope that did not exist — deleting the column would have settled that row's question in the other direction. `TestTheInboxIsScopedToTheWorkspaceExceptWhereItIsNot` asserts both halves and was shown red twice: with no scoping at all, and with the `IS NULL` half removed, which hid every organization-level notification and is the failure the row's fix note predicted. |
| F44 | M32.9 | No account deletion or GDPR erasure exists anywhere, while the schema and four other sites describe both as behaviour that exists — and two tables holding email addresses have no deletion path of any kind | `internal/store/migrations/00200_identity.sql:35-37` (`anonymized_at`, *"Set by the GDPR erasure routine"*), and `users` / `destination_disputes` | `anonymized_at` has no writer: grep returns the column, the sqlc model field and three `SELECT *` scan lists. `users` appears in none of the 14 `DELETE FROM` statements and nothing writes `users.deleted_at`. Four further sites assert deletion as existing: `docs/SECURITY.md:51`, `internal/audit/audit.go:277`, `01200_invitations.sql:38` and `01600_destination_disputes.sql:56`. The same migration marks `mfa_secret` *"Phase 3"* four lines earlier, so the file knows how to mark something forward-looking and did not here. Two corrections to the finder's enumeration: `audit_logs` **is** purgeable — it is in `PartitionedTables` and governed by `NewRetentionPolicy`, just defaulted to 0 and documented that way in three places — and `invitations` and `notifications` **do** cascade from `organizations`/`users`. What is genuinely undeletable is `users`, and `destination_disputes`, which by explicit design has *no foreign keys at all* (`01600:49-52`) and no purge. `reserved_aliases` also lacks a purge but holds no personal data | **Low to moderate.** Nothing exploitable. Its cost is that the project's own convention — every deferral is written down — has a hole exactly where a compliance reader would look: erasure appears in neither Plan.md's scope rows nor *Not in Phase 2*. `Plan.md:426`'s framing (*"the largest table … is out of scope for subject-access and erasure requests"*) reads as though the other tables are in scope | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 2, minor to moderate. Closes as a **record and not a build** — the finding is that erasure is described by five sites and implemented by none, and saying so is the fix, the way [F141](#open) said it. Building erasure is a feature and belongs in Plan.md first. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. **Recorded, not built**, as the triage decided: writing an erasure subsystem inside a phase close is a feature and belongs in Plan.md first. All five sites that described erasure in the present tense are corrected — `00200_identity.sql`'s *"set by the GDPR erasure routine"* on a column with no writer, `00400_analytics.sql`'s scope sentence which was true of its own table and read as a claim about the others, `00600_phase2_dormant.sql`'s *"GDPR erasure overwrites the label"*, `internal/audit/audit.go`'s *"after the account is deleted"*, and `docs/SECURITY.md`'s copy of the same. The absence itself is now in the two lists a reader consults — Plan.md's *Not in Phase 2* and README's *Not built yet* — and has its own entry under SECURITY.md's *What is not defended*, each naming what **is** reclaimed (partitioned analytics, audit retention, invitation and notification cascades) so the gap is sized rather than gestured at. The row's own correction to the finder's enumeration is preserved: `audit_logs` is purgeable and the cascades do work. |
| F57 | M32.9 | The shared rate limiter writes the client's full IPv4 address into a Redis key, and nothing tells an operator pointing at a managed Redis that they are now storing addresses | `internal/ratelimit/ratelimit.go:299-310` (`Key`), composed at `internal/ratelimit/shared.go:175` | Confirmed live, read-only, on the running instance: `redis-cli --scan --pattern 'lc:rl:*'` → `lc:rl:api:172.18.0.1`, TTL 48s of a computed 120s. Two counters land and reframe it: a Redis key is not a column, so the stance (*"No IP column anywhere"*) is not literally violated — every corroborating doc scopes itself to storage; and the `/64` for IPv6 is evasion resistance, not privacy (`Key`'s own doc: *"Handing out /64s to single hosts is normal"*), so anonymising the v4 side to /24 is **not** an available fix — it would let one host throttle 255 neighbours. Keyed hashing is the only coherent fix. What survives: the shipped compose is memory-only, but `LINKCTRL_REDIS_URL` is operator-configurable and managed Redis commonly snapshots by default | **Low.** Bounded to 120 seconds in a store the design treats as disposable. The actionable half is documentation: the privacy stance should say what it does about Redis, and `docs/deployment.md`'s managed-Redis guidance should tell operators to disable persistence | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. Closes as a **recorded limitation and not code** — its own fix note says keyed hashing is the only coherent repair, and the actionable half is telling an operator pointing at managed Redis what is stored there and for how long. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. **Documentation, which the row itself says is the actionable half.** The inherited privacy stance now states its own exception: it is about storage, and the shared limiter's Redis key carries the full IPv4 address for the limiter's window. `docs/deployment.md` tells an operator pointing at a managed Redis to disable persistence there, with the reason — a service snapshotting by default writes client addresses to somebody else's disk on a retention schedule the operator did not choose. Both texts say why anonymising the v4 side is **not** available: a `/24` key would let one host exhaust 255 neighbours' budget, and the `/64` used for IPv6 is evasion resistance rather than privacy. Keyed hashing is named as the only coherent fix and is not built. |
| F58 | M32.9 | `SeedBlocklist` accepts malformed `LINKCTRL_DESTINATION_BLOCKLIST` entries silently, inserting rows that can never match | `internal/link/blocking.go:688-708` — lowercase, trim, insert; `checkListEntry` is never called | Reproduced: `HostCandidates` only ever produces dot-delimited fragments of a real hostname, so a stored `https://evil.example` or `*.evil.example` row is unreachable by `MatchBlockedDestination`. Two narrowings. The panic-on-malformed promise at `blocking.go:170-173` is scoped, in both places it appears, to the **embedded** list — generalising it to the env list is inference. And it is not a regression: the pre-M30 in-memory implementation validated nothing either, which is what m30.md's only claim here (*"the env list keeps working"*) promises. The implied fix is also wrong: calling `checkListEntry` would reject IP-literal entries that currently work | **Low.** Operator-authored and self-inflicted; it cannot make the instance refuse less than before M30. The cost is silence — the operator sees their entry in the table and it refuses nothing — one function call away from the boot-time rigour `main.go:508-517` spends on exactly this failure mode | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. Closes as a **recorded limitation and not code** — calling `checkListEntry` would reject IP-literal entries that work today, which this row's own evidence names. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Recorded at `SeedBlocklist` and in `docs/configuration.md`, where an operator writing the variable will meet it: bare hostnames, not URLs or wildcards, because `https://evil.example` and `*.evil.example` pass the host fold, land in the table, appear in the list and match nothing. **Calling `checkListEntry` is the wrong fix and the comment says so**: it refuses IP-literal entries, which work today and which an operator blocking `169.254.169.254` reasonably expects to — so the cheap repair trades a silent no-op for a silent regression. Also not a regression from anything: the pre-M30 in-memory list validated nothing either. |
| F113 | M44.9 | Nothing reaps an unverified hostname claim, so a registration holds a name instance-wide forever | `internal/link/verification.go:339-343` skips rows with `VerifiedAt == nil`; `domains_hostname_key` (`00300_links.sql:23`) is instance-wide and unconditioned on verification | Narrowed sharply by verification: the squat itself is a **documented limitation** — `Plan.md:815` states *"a second registration of the same name is refused instance-wide, whoever asks… no way to hand one over"* — and the uninformative refusal is deliberate and reasoned in-tree. The index is partial on `deleted_at IS NULL`, so deleting the domain does free the name, but only the holder can delete it. What is genuinely undocumented is that an unverified claim is never polled out of existence, so the squat is free and permanent rather than merely unhandable | Low. No confidentiality or integrity loss; denial of a name on one instance, and the operator's own hostnames are configured statically rather than through this table. Fix note: a reaper on a timer breaks the case `verification.go:339-343` protects — a hostname registered before DNS cut-over — and must not treat a NULL `verification_checked_at` as evidence of abandonment, since `RenameDomain` writes that NULL on a live row | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. Closes as a **recorded limitation and not code** — a reaper on unverified claims breaks the pre-DNS-cut-over case `verification.go:339-343` protects. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Recorded at `verificationWorkList`, which is the function that skips these rows, and in Plan.md's hostname limitation beside the squat it already documented. The finding is the half that was **not** documented: the claim is not merely unhandable, it is permanent, because nothing polls an unverified registration out of existence. **A reaper is not the fix it looks like** — the pending arm exists to keep checking a hostname registered before its DNS cut-over, which is exactly what a reaper would delete, and it could not treat a NULL `verification_checked_at` as abandonment because `RenameDomain` writes one onto a live row. |
| F117 | M44.9 | `canAdminister`'s organization limb authorizes an organization-owned domain from the identity's permission union rather than the covering membership | `internal/link/domains.go:131-143`, the `organizationID != nil` limb, which compares only `actor.OrgID` after a bare `actor.Can(PermDomainsWrite)` | This is D44's rule unapplied — *"a write is authorized by the membership whose scope covers the object being written, not by the identity's union"* — so a workspace-scoped admin's union would reach an organization-owned row. Latency verified on four independent checks: `CreateDomain`'s only caller writes both owner columns, no update statement touches `workspace_id`, the seeded default row has a NULL organization, and `domains.workspace_id` is `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so deleting a workspace removes the row rather than promoting it | Low and latent — the state is unconstructible through any shipped or migrated path, and the CHECK at `02500_domain_ownership.sql:48-52` is what legalises it. It becomes real the moment anything registers an organization-owned hostname, and whoever adds that surface has no reason to look here. Fix note: replacing all three limbs with the organization-wide check is wrong — only the middle limb is D44's, the workspace limb is correctly the union under D31, and the instance-default limb is F70 and D38's unanswered question about who administers an instance. Also `Domains()` calls this per row, so a per-call authority load is a query per row | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 7, low. Closes as a **recorded limitation and not code** — the state is unconstructible through any shipped or migrated path, and the fix note rules out replacing all three limbs. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. Recorded in `canAdminister`, beside the limb it is about, because the row's own point is that whoever adds an organization-owned hostname surface has no reason to look here. The comment carries the four checks that make it latent rather than live — `CreateDomain`'s only caller writes both owner columns, no update statement touches `workspace_id`, the seeded default has a NULL organization, and `workspace_id` is `ON DELETE CASCADE` so deleting a workspace removes the row rather than promoting it — and why the obvious repair is wrong: replacing all three limbs takes the workspace limb with it, and that one is correctly the union under D31. Untouched by [D100](../../Plan.md#phase-2-decisions-taken-after-the-plan-was-finalised), which moved the *instance-default* limb and left this one exactly as filed. |
| F70 | M39 | `domains.write` is a **role** permission, so on an instance with more than one organization every organization's owner and admin can administer the **instance-wide** default domain — its root redirect and its bot policy — not only their own hostnames | `internal/store/migrations/00800_domain_root_redirect.sql:32-34` grants the slug to the owner and admin role ids with no organization scope; `internal/link/domains.go`, `canAdminister`'s third limb returns `domains.write` alone for a row with both owner columns NULL; `internal/link/domain_settings.go:77` and `:191` guard `SetRootRedirect` and `SetBotBlocking` on the bare permission | Named while writing M39's ownership check, which had to decide what the instance default's limb should be. m39.md says the default "**stays** administrable only by instance owner/admin", so preserving the existing guard is what the milestone asked for and M39 neither widens nor narrows it — registering a hostname is not a route to it, and the new collection refuses to rename or delete the default at all. The reach is nonetheless real and already documented from the other end: `Plan.md`'s known-limitation row for the dispute queue calls it "the shape `domains.write` already has", `docs/SECURITY.md:174` repeats it, and migration `01600_destination_disputes.sql:100-105` excluded admin from `destinations.review` for exactly this reason. Recorded rather than fixed because narrowing it is a change to who administers an instance — the missing instance-level principal (D38) — rather than a defect in per-domain ownership | **Minor**, and unreachable on a single-organization instance, which is every default one. It becomes real with `LINKCTRL_SIGNUP_MODE=open`, where one registration provisions an organization and makes the registrant its owner: they then hold `domains.write` and can repoint the root of the hostname every workspace's links are served on. The same registration already reaches `destination_disputes`' sibling gap (F15), so the two want one answer rather than two | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 3, minor — a principal can act on it, in scope by the 2026-08-04 standing rule. **Blocked on D38**, the missing instance-level principal that also blocked F15 and F31 on the first triage. D98 and `lctl instance principal move` moved that ground since D38 was written, so the question returns in a different shape rather than staying refused, and it is put when this row is reached. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05, [D100](../../Plan.md#phase-2-decisions-taken-after-the-plan-was-finalised), owner-answered. A new permission, `domains.write.instance` (migration `03500`), granted to **no role** and reaching a person only through `instance_grants` — so the instance default domain's root redirect and bot policy are the instance principal's. `domains.write` is untouched and still the ownership check over a workspace's own hostnames, which is M39's point and was never what this row was about. D18 limb recorded as the inherited rule requires: **neither** — repointing the root escalates nothing, is reversible, and discloses nothing, so it is delegable and deliberately absent from `NonDelegableScopes`. Also absent from `InstanceGrantable`: the principal holds it and may not confer it, because what D98 decided the principal may delegate is instance-level *review*. **Two things surfaced while building it.** `canAdminister`'s instance-default limb is now checked first, before the role permission, since checking `domains.write` up front let a workspace admin past the gate and left the answer to the switch — the arrangement that made this reachable. And `domainForWrite`'s *this is the instance default* refusal moved **above** the permission check: it is a fact about the row rather than the actor, nobody may rename or delete it including the principal, and until now every owner passed the permission check and reached it anyway, so the two orders were indistinguishable. The migration confers the scope on **whoever already holds the principal** rather than re-deriving the earliest account, so a principal moved with `lctl instance principal move` stays moved. `TestTheInstanceRootIsThePrincipalsAndNotAnOrganizationOwners` replaces the old *needs domains.write* test, whose premise D100 falsified — instance grants survive a demotion, so demoting the fixture owner no longer removes the capability — and it asserts a full organization **owner** is refused, which is the finding stated as a test. Shown red by reverting the guard. |
| F12 | M28.5 | D18's text does not describe the map it governs, in two ways: applied literally its two limbs would make an irreversible permission delegable, and its *"either direction"* sentence promises a disarming the code never performs | [Plan.md](../../Plan.md#phase-2-decisions-taken-after-the-plan-was-finalised)'s D18 row, against `auth.NonDelegableScopes` at `internal/auth/apikey.go:186` and the comment stating the wider rule at `:131-143`; enforcement at `resolveScopes` (`internal/auth/apikey.go:607`, the mint path) and `narrowScopes` (`internal/auth/apikey_rotation.go:320-338`, the rotation path), against `Authenticate` (`:515-598`), whose `identity.restrictTo(row.Scopes)` at `:589` re-filters on the owner's role and nothing else | Found while recording `org.delete`'s delegability, which m28.5.md required be named against D18. D18 says a permission is non-delegable "when reading it exposes an actor's identity tied to network data, **or** when holding it lets a key widen its own reach. Everything else is delegable." `org.delete` does neither — it discloses nothing and removes tenancy rather than granting any — yet it has been in `NonDelegableScopes` since Phase 1's `00700_seed.sql`, on irreversibility. The map's own comment states the wider rule explicitly: "the rule this map encodes is now 'escalating, irreversible, or disclosing' rather than only the first two." So the code and the decision text disagree about what the rule *is*, and the code is the one that has been right since before D18 was written (2026-07-31). **Folded in from F56 (M32.9), 2026-08-04:** the same decision's other sentence is wrong the other way. D18 says *"Flipping a permission in **either direction** stays a one-line map edit"*, and it does not: adding an already-granted permission to `NonDelegableScopes` would not disarm keys already issued, because `Authenticate` restricts the identity to the **row's** stored scopes and never consults the map. Never exploitable — `git log -S` confirms all the entries landed in the same commit as the migration creating their permission (`a0f23ff` for `audit.read`/00900, `e8a6b1c` for `destinations.review`/01600, the Phase-1 seed for the rest), so no key has ever held a scope that later became non-delegable. F56's own "only enforcement" reading is now **one site out of date** and is corrected here rather than carried forward: M44's rotation added a second check, `narrowScopes` at `apikey_rotation.go:328-338`, which refuses to carry a now-non-delegable scope into a successor. Mint and rotate are covered; request time is not. F56's cited claim at `apikey.go:147-149` was an over-read — that comment is about the *deleting* direction and is true there. | Minor today, and latent rather than active: no permission is mis-classified in the tree, and `org.delete` is correctly non-delegable. The cost is the next milestone that adds an irreversible permission — M44's rotation scopes are the near candidate — applying D18's two limbs as written, finding neither matches, and concluding the scope is delegable. Correcting it means rewording a decision, which is the owner's, and D18 is referenced by name from six milestone files. Absorbs F56's limb, which was filed **Note / informational** and stays that: no defect in shipped behaviour, and the fix is either an accurate one-line correction to D18 or an `isNonDelegable` filter in `restrictTo` as belt-and-braces — the helper already exists at `internal/auth/apikey.go:675-678` and is called from the two write paths but not from the read one. Both limbs are corrections to the same decision's text, which is why they are one row. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 5, minor — one-edit corrections. **[F56](#closed) (M32.9) was merged into this row** by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04: both convict D18's wording against the same map, and one reword answers both. **[F104](#open) was deliberately not merged** although it calls itself *"F12's shape a second time"* — same shape, different rule, different file, different code: correcting D18 does nothing for `phase-details/README.md:75`, and merging on shape alone would put two unrelated repairs behind one disposition. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05, owner-answered. **The text is corrected and the map is untouched**, which is the direction this row argued for: the code has been right since before D18 was written. Limb one now reads *escalating, irreversible, or disclosing* — the three-limb rule `NonDelegableScopes`'s own comment has stated all along — so `org.delete` is described by the rule that has governed it since Phase 1. Limb two is **narrowed rather than deleted**: adding a scope to the map binds keys minted and rotated afterwards and does not disarm one already issued, because `Authenticate` restricts to the row's stored scopes and never consults the map. The request-time gap is **documented and left open**, with the price of closing it stated — an `isNonDelegable` filter in `restrictTo` is a check on every authenticated request for a state no key has ever been in. Both corrections are marked in place in the D18 row rather than replacing it, and the near miss is recorded at the map too: the next milestone to add an irreversible permission is the one that would have applied the two limbs, found neither matched, and shipped it delegable. Absorbs F56's limb. No `D` number: decisions.md is append-only and a later entry correcting an earlier one is what it is for. |

| F16 | M32 | Most dashboard pages are counted as redirects in the HTTP metrics, because the surface classifier's prefix list stopped being updated after Phase 1 | `internal/observability/metrics.go` — `webPrefixes`, and `ClassifySurface`'s fall-through, which returns `SurfaceRedirect` for anything it does not recognise | Noticed while adding `/feeds` and finding there was no list to add it to that anybody had been maintaining. `webPrefixes` holds eight entries — `/login`, `/logout`, `/setup`, `/dashboard`, `/docs`, `/links`, `/keys`, `/account` — and `ClassifySurface` labels everything else that is not `/api/`, `/static/` or an ops endpoint as `redirect`. The cost lands on the numbers an operator reads: `linkctrl_http_requests_total{surface="redirect"}` and `linkctrl_http_request_duration_seconds{surface="redirect"}` mix short-link traffic with dashboard page loads whose budget is 250ms rather than 20ms, so the redirect surface's own figures are inflated by whatever the dashboard is doing. The SLO itself is unaffected — that is measured by `linkctrl_redirect_duration_seconds`, which the redirect handler records directly and which this does not touch — so the claim in docs/slo.md stands. Not fixed here because adding `/feeds` alone would make the list wrong in a new way, and the honest fix is to derive the classifier from `dashboardPatterns` rather than to extend a second hand-written copy of it, which is a change to a shipped metric's labels. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 5, minor — one-edit corrections. **Remedy pointer corrected 2026-08-04, F85's fix:** this row's evidence names `dashboardPatterns` in `internal/httpx/router.go` as "the list that *is* maintained" and proposes deriving the classifier from it. That slice no longer exists — F85 deleted it, because it had fallen behind the registrations it sat beside. The source this row asks for is now `(*appMux).mounts()`, which is derived from what the application mux was actually handed rather than hand-written, so it is a better one than the row could have had. The finding itself is unchanged and still open. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. The classifier is **derived, not extended**. `observability.SetWebPaths` is called at boot with `(*appMux).mounts()` — what the application mux was actually handed — so the dashboard set cannot fall behind the routes again. The row proposed deriving it from `dashboardPatterns`, which F85 had since deleted for having fallen behind in exactly this way; `mounts()` is the better source it could not have known about, and its own Reviewed cell had already recorded that correction. Adding `/feeds` to the hand-written list would have made the list wrong in a new way, which is why the row refused to do it. `TestEveryDashboardRouteIsCountedAsTheDashboard` drives its assertion from `mounts()` too, so the test cannot drift either — a list checked against a copy of itself is what this finding was. Shown red by removing the boot call: nine routes reported `surface="redirect"`, `/automation` and `/campaigns` and `/disputes` among them. |

| F41 | M32.9 | `CacheKeyVersion` was not bumped when M32.5 added bot-policy fields, so a snapshot written by a pre-M32.5 binary reads as "do not block" for up to `REDIRECT_TTL` | `internal/redirect/snapshot.go:44-50` and `internal/redirect/resolver.go:24` | Reproduced at the Redis layer: with `{"block_bots":true}` in Postgres, dropping the in-process copy and overwriting the shared entry with the payload a pre-M32.5 binary would write (identical minus the `omitempty` `bb`/`db`) yielded **three 302s to a bot**, and the app never revalidates or rewrites the entry. Half the finding is dead, though: `SetBotBlocking` calls `InvalidateDomain` unconditionally, which sweeps `lc:a:v1:<domain>:*`, so entries written **before** blocking is switched on have a zero stale window. What survives needs an old binary writing entries **while blocking is already on** — reachable because `docs/releasing.md:102` runs migrations at boot before the listener opens, so a rolling restart has old and new containers serving concurrently, and `kindDomain` is new in `fa0a079`, so an old replica hits `apply`'s `default:` branch and neither clears its tier nor stops writing bot-less entries. `docs/releasing.md:120-124` calls in-series rollback *"safe"* and cross-series residue *"harmless"* | **Low.** Fail-open on an opt-in control whose failure mode is the pre-M32.5 status quo — no disclosure, no authz bypass — but silent, bounded only by a 24h TTL, on a sequence the release documentation calls safe. The row is right that the comment's justification is false: *"nobody can have switched blocking on yet"* assumes the old build stops before the new one starts, and migrations run at boot before the listener opens, so a rolling restart has both serving at once. But the residue is empty by arithmetic — 0.1.0 is the only release that exists and it keys on `v1`, this build keys on `v3`, and M32.5, M34 and M36 all land inside the same unreleased minor — so no entry any released build wrote is one this build can read. Same shape as [F132](#closed): the argument stays untried, the population is empty. What the comment now carries is the **rule for next time**, which is the part that would otherwise have licensed the same mistake: a new omitempty field may skip the bump only when its zero value means the same thing to a visitor as the true value would, never because nobody has had time to configure it. |

A nil `*slog.Logger` panics inside `slog.(*Logger).Enabled`, which is what the M35 signature test hit before the accessor existed — the connection was closed mid-response and the client saw `EOF`. Production always sets `Logger`, so the reachable case is a fixture or an embedder of `httpx`; the panic is nonetheless on the hot path's 503 branch, i.e. the branch that runs when Postgres is already unwell | Minor. Unreachable in `cmd/linkctrl`, which always sets the field, and every failure branch is one a healthy instance does not take. Both raw dereferences now go through the nil-tolerant `log()` accessor M35 added for exactly this reason and then did not retrofit — `redirect.go:230`'s 503 branch and the sampled success line at `:414`. Held by `TestNothingOnTheRedirectPathDereferencesLoggerDirectly`, a scan of the package's `redirect*.go` for `.Logger.<method>(…)`, because the accessor on its own is what already existed and was already being gone round; what makes it hold is that nothing may bypass it. | F9 | M26.6 | An uncached redirect pays `REDIS_READ_TIMEOUT` twice while Redis is stalled, not once | `internal/redirect/resolver.go` — `fromRedis` (the failed `Get`) and `store` (the `Set` that repopulates), both reached synchronously from `Resolve` | Measured for M26.6 on 2026-07-31, against `redisProxy.blackholed(true)` with a client built the way `internal/platform/redis.Open` builds one (`DialTimeout` 1s, `ReadTimeout` 50ms). A cold `Resolve` took **108ms**; the two following resolves took 0s, because the in-process tier was populated. The 108ms is 50ms of failed lookup, plus the Postgres query, plus 50ms of `Set` against a server that will not answer it either. Both calls are bounded and neither retries, so this is not the compounding M26.6 was scheduled for — that was the invalidation path's three-attempt loop, now bounded — but the `redis.go` comment claiming a stalled Redis "costs a little latency" was written about one call and there are two. | Minor. Nothing is wrong, only slow, and only while Redis is stalled: the redirect is still served from Postgres. The documented uncached target is 100ms and a stalled cache puts a cold redirect just past it, so the cost is an SLO the operator cannot meet during a Redis stall rather than a failure. The obvious fix — skip the repopulating `Set` when the lookup just timed out — is a change to the redirect path and therefore owes the k6 re-run, which is why it is not taken inside a milestone that deliberately left that path alone. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 4, minor — redirect path and cache. The batch owes one k6 re-run, not six. Deliberately **not merged** with [F98](#open) by M45's queue hygiene, 2026-08-04. Same class — a stalled Redis costing `REDIS_READ_TIMEOUT` on the redirect path, against a document that says it costs nothing — but different call sites (`resolver.go`'s failed `Get` and repopulating `Set` versus `analytics/returning.go`'s `SISMEMBER`), different cache tiers, different SLOs (100ms uncached, 20ms cached), different false sentences, and incompatible fixes: this row's is to skip the repopulating `Set`, and F98's own note rules out both of the obvious ones. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. The repopulating `Set` is skipped when the lookup that led to it just failed for any reason other than the key being absent — a server that will not answer a `Get` will not usefully answer the `Set`. Recorded on the resolver rather than threaded through the call, because the stall is a property of the server and the write happens inside a singleflight whose leader may be a different request than the one that saw it; the window is `DBTimeout`, one uncached resolve, which is exactly the span between the failed read and the write it provokes. **Deliberately not a circuit breaker**: reads are never skipped, because a read is how the cache recovers. The in-process tier is still populated, so skipping costs only that the shared tier stays cold for a window in which it was cold anyway. `TestAStalledRedisIsPaidForOncePerResolve` runs against a listener that accepts and never answers, at a 400ms timeout so the two outcomes are hundreds of milliseconds apart rather than tens; shown red by removing the skip, at *400.869753ms*. A second test asserts the suppression lapses, so one transient stall does not stop the shared tier being written to for the life of the process. |

The framing that the SLO was never measured on this path is **refuted** — `docs/slo.md:230-243` records all 240,002 cache-hit requests performing the lookup and matching, at 100% under 20ms — so what is wrong is the stall case and the two sentences, not the measurement | Minor, and the same class and tier as F9, which covers only the two uncached calls and predates M34. Bounded, which changes the emphasis: the path exists **only on split-host deployments** (`registerRoot` is called solely inside the `SplitHosts()` branch), the query is a unique-partial-index lookup on a table holding one row, and `WriteTimeout` (30s) does bound it. So the singleflight half is near-costless — a few duplicate index lookups per TTL, not a herd. The half that matters is the pool crossing: `main.go:278-280` says the redirect pool exists *"so a slow analytics query on the application pool cannot leave a redirect waiting to acquire a connection"*, and this is a redirect-tree path acquiring from `pools.App`. M23 makes the miss fleet-simultaneous, since `Subscriber.flush()` clears every replica's root cache on every re-establishment including the first | **Minor.** A stated architectural guarantee with a real hole, biting only on split-host under app-pool exhaustion, blast radius one URL rather than the alias traffic carrying the SLO | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 4, minor — redirect path and cache. Rate limiting slows a sweep but does not remove the signal. The behaviour is not new — the endpoint has answered `409` for a taken address since Phase 1 — but until M29 the endpoint was API-only and there was no browser form; opening self-serve signup is what makes it a surface a stranger reaches. M27 deliberately spent argon2 work to stop redemption answering the same question (D27), so the product already treats this as something worth not disclosing, and the two surfaces now disagree. | Minor, and bounded by the mode: on a `closed` or `invite` instance the endpoint refuses before it looks anything up, and the shipped default is `closed`. On a deliberately open instance it lets somebody test a leaked address list for membership. The honest fix is to answer `202` either way and mail "you already have an account" to a taken address, which is a behaviour change to a documented status code and a second mail template — scope, not a detail, which is why it is deferred rather than taken inside M29. | **Approved as work 2026-08-05**, M45's third triage — Tier 3, minor — a principal can act on it, in scope by the 2026-08-04 standing rule. | **[M45](phase-details/m45.md)**, 2026-08-05. A taken address now gets the same `202`, the same body and the same argon2 cost as a free one, and the answer goes to the address by mail — `account-exists`, a fourth template — rather than to whoever typed it into the form. The hash is computed **before** the lookup, deliberately: argon2 is this request by two orders of magnitude, so branching first would have left the timing carrying what the status code stopped carrying. Nothing is written for a taken address, which also closes a smaller thing the row did not name — a pending registration would have superseded whatever the real owner had outstanding, so a stranger could invalidate their link by typing their address in. `TestRegistrationCannotBeAskedWhetherAnAddressIsTaken` compares whole response bodies with only the echoed address and the clock normalized out, **and that is what caught the residue**: the taken branch returned Go's nanosecond clock where the free one returned a value Postgres had truncated to a microsecond, so the fractional digits answered the question. Truncated to match. The 409 is gone from `api/openapi.yaml`, and the unreachable branch in `web_signup.go` is deleted rather than left asserting the opposite of the behaviour — its text also offered a password reset this product has never had ([F141](#open)). |

It is recorded because the *obvious* repair is the one D98 forbids — an operation that confers `instance.admin` would let a principal mint another principal, and the delegation bound exists precisely to stop the set of delegators growing. Any in-product recovery path is a variation on that, so this needs a decision rather than a fix. A CLI command, `lctl` acting on the same filesystem-access trust `/setup` claims, is the shape most likely to be right | Approved as work 2026-08-05 — the owner's review of the rows M45's own fix work produced, **and the owner chose the shape: an `lctl` command**, which is what this row's severity cell nominates. Fixed under **M45's own number**. | **M45**, `internal/instance` and a new `cmd/lctl/instance.go`. `lctl instance principal show` names who administers the box; `lctl instance principal move --to <email>` hands it over. **It moves and cannot add**: the transaction confers all four `InstancePrincipalScopes` on the target, withdraws all four from every other holder, and re-reads the holders of `instance.admin` before committing — anything other than exactly the named account refuses and changes nothing. That check is [D98](decisions.md)'s delegation bound asserted rather than claimed, and it is why the recovery does not undermine the thing it recovers. `Service.MovePrincipal` takes **no actor and makes no permission check**, because the authority is filesystem access to the box — the claim `internal/auth`'s `Register` already rests the whole setup flow on — and asking for a `--user` to act as would be asking the operator which identity to pretend to be. The absence of a check is safe only while nothing routes there, so `TestThePrincipalMoveIsReachableFromTheShellAndNowhereElse` scans the tree and fails the build on the symbol appearing outside `internal/instance` and `cmd/lctl`. It is guarded like `lctl seed` and `lctl demo` — refused under `APP_ENV=production` without `--force` — and audited instance-wide with the actor recorded as `system`, since nobody signed in to do it. **No migration**: the four queries it needs already existed. Reviewers already appointed keep the queue. Three integration tests and two CLI tests, every one sabotaged red and restored by counter-edit. |
